Verification method.** While proving the four-total assess form reproduced the old deck numbers (#306/#336), two old single-year decks — plans **91** and **98**, generated six minutes apart on the evening of 2026-08-14 — exported at **27 slides with no year-1 appendix**. That directly contradicted the code, which in single-year mode dropped **only** `slide29.xml`, leaving 28. Two samples agreeing was enough to produce a confident and completely wrong conclusion — *"the old code had a latent bug and #374's '28 slides' is stale doc"* — which was published in chat before a **third** sample overturned it: plan **83**, same code, same single-year mode, exported at **28 WITH** the appendix. The code and the doc were right all along; plans 91 and 98 had almost certainly been **hand-edited in Google Slides after generation**, which is entirely rational — on a single-year plan the year-1 appendix just repeats the combined view, so whoever prepped those two decks deleted the redundant slide. **The generator shares every deck `role=writer, type=anyone` deliberately (#336), so "someone changed it" is not an edge case, it is the documented state of every file it has ever produced.** The rules that follow: to establish what the code emits, **generate a fresh artifact yourself or read the handler** — a stored artifact is corroboration, never the baseline; when stored artifacts disagree with the code, **the code wins until three or more agree against it**, and two agreeing samples from the same sitting are one event, not two; and state a finding like this as provisional until the sample is wide enough, because a wrong "the docs are stale" claim invites someone to "fix" a correct doc. **The reliable half, worth keeping:** Google's `/export/pptx` is FAITHFUL — the two-year deck exported all 29 slides with both appendix slides and their `$0` year-2 figures intact — so exporting a deck **you just generated** is a sound way to verify slide count, token substitution and every rendered number without opening PowerPoint.

**411. A bell's TITLE is the clear contract, which means its RECIPIENT is FREE — you can re-address a live action-required bell without touching a single clear site (2026-08-17). ALWAYS-APPLIES (cross-repo / bells).** The inverse of #357/#407, and it is the half nobody states. Because `clearHlmConfirmBells` matches on **pipeline + client_id + `dismissible:false` + `read:false` + title PREFIX** and never reads `recipient`, moving the *"Detailed tax plan meeting confirmation email"* step from the allocated Tax Planner to Tray was a change to who the bell is ADDRESSED to and to nothing else: both fires kept **byte-identical titles**, and the one clear site retires **both vintages** — the planner-addressed bells already in flight and the Tray-addressed ones minted after — with no migration, no dual-clear branch and no cutover window. **The rule: recipient is data, title is API.** Re-addressing is cheap and safe; re-wording is a contract break that strands every open bell with no clear site left. So when a step changes OWNER, change `default_recipients` and the handler's `defaultRecipients`, and **leave the title alone even when it now names the wrong role** — a historically-wrong-sounding title that still clears beats an accurate one that cannot. The same logic protects the rule KEY: `TAX_planner_hlm_ready` and `TAX_roi_skipped_hlm_ready` kept names that now read as lies, because renaming a key orphans its Notification Editor row and every code reference, and the key is not what routes the bell. **Do still check for open bells at cutover** — here there were none, so the question was moot, but a title-preserving re-address only guarantees the bells stay CLEARABLE, not that the previous recipient stops seeing the ones they already hold.

**412. Once handlers auto-write a generic status, the closed-reason ladder must test every SPECIFIC arm BEFORE the generic one — or the specific reason silently disappears (2026-08-17). Tax track / display correctness.** `client_tax_plans.status` used to have exactly one writer (the Live/Stopped switch), so `status === 'stopped'` genuinely meant "a human flicked this switch" and its `"Plan stopped"` arm could sit anywhere in the ladder. The moment five handlers began writing `status='stopped'` **on the same UPDATE that records the terminal outcome**, that stopped being true: every deposit-refunded, declined and refunded plan now ALSO satisfies the generic test. With the bare arm anywhere but last, `"Stopped — deposit refunded"`, `"Client declined"` and `"Stopped — retainer refunded"` all collapse to `"Plan stopped"`, and — the part that makes it hard to catch — **only the hand-flicked plans, the ones nobody was worried about, keep their correct wording.** The fix is one line of ordering in `utils/tax-plan-steps.ts`'s `closedReason`, with the comment explaining why, but the general shape is worth carrying: **when you add a new writer to a column that a display ladder already tests, re-read the ladder in writer order, not in reading order.** A newly-broadened predicate does not announce itself — it just starts matching earlier than the arm that carries the real information. Related: the backfill for this change copied the ladder **arm-for-arm** rather than re-deriving from column names, which is the only way the stored status and the displayed reason can agree.

**413. `notifyByRule` recipient resolution: the rule's `recipients` override wins ONLY when it is non-empty AND resolvable — an unresolvable token falls through to the code's `defaultRecipients` and can never silently drop a bell (2026-08-17). Notifications / routing.** Two columns look interchangeable and are not. `default_recipients` is the **Notification Editor's DISPLAY** of what the handler passes; `recipients` is the **override**. What actually routes a bell is the handler's `defaultRecipients` argument unless `recipients` both holds something and resolves. Dynamic tokens like `ALLOCATED_TAX_PLANNER` only resolve when the CALL SITE passes a matching `dynamic` map — the direct analogue of #324, where a role token in a template does nothing unless the handler puts it in ctx. **The practical consequence, and why the 2026-08-17 re-address could leave `recipients` untouched:** even if a stale planner token were sitting in that column, the call sites pass no `dynamic` map for it, so it resolves to nothing and the code default (Tray) wins — the failure mode is "falls back", never "sends to nobody". That fallback is the safe direction and is worth preserving deliberately; do not "harden" it into a throw. **The audit rule: to learn who receives a bell, read the HANDLER's `defaultRecipients` and then check `recipients` for a live override — never read `default_recipients` alone, which is documentation, not routing.** Verified in the live data: both rules carry `recipients = NULL` and `default_recipients = ["tvaldes@elitert.com"]`, and the bell minted during click-testing landed on `tvaldes@elitert.com`.

**414. Rows MIGRATED from the legacy system never got their branch column backfilled — so a step machine that keys a whole branch on that one column reports every migrated row as stuck weeks in the past, and looks correct on every row you would think to test (2026-08-18). ALWAYS-APPLIES (tax track / migrated data).** `buildTaxPlanSteps` decided "has this plan passed the Tax 3 decision?" from `client_tax_plans.tax_decision` alone (plus the two Undecided/ExtraMeeting variants). **Sixteen plans carry `legacy_source='old-system'` and a NULL `tax_decision`** — they were migrated *at* whatever stage they had reached, and the migration wrote the state columns it had (`agreement_sent`, `client_signed`, `retainer_status`, `post_review_decision`) without synthesising the decision that logically preceded them. So Jeff Titus (plan 105) and Marc Ehrhardt (plan 89), both signed and paid and deep into Tax 5, had Client Overview naming a **Tax 2** step as their next action. The fix is a second, evidence-based predicate OR-ed into the branch: `pastTax3 = agreement_sent === "Yes" || client_signed === "Yes" || retainer_status === "succeeded" || post_review_decision != null` — **any one of which is only writable by code that runs AFTER the decision**, so it cannot false-positive.

**Why it hid for months, and the general rule.** The branch was right on every plan created by the app, because the app always writes the decision before anything downstream — the bug is invisible unless your sample includes migrated rows, and nobody click-tests a two-year-old plan. **A branch column is a RECORD of a decision; the columns written after it are EVIDENCE that it happened. Prove the branch with the evidence, because the record can be missing while the evidence cannot.** Corollary for any future import: an importer that fills "where the record got to" without back-filling "how it got there" is not wrong — it is the normal shape of a migration — so every predicate keying on a single decision column must be read as *"…or the row predates us"*. When auditing, run the branch test against `legacy_source IS NOT NULL` rows specifically and see whether the answers are plausible. **A second consequence, easy to miss:** `pastTax3` also had to SUPPRESS the new fork windows (a plan factually past Tax 3 must never re-fork into lanes it has already walked) and to bound the team-member rule, so broadening a branch predicate means re-reading every *other* rule that assumed the old, narrower answer. Cross-ref **#339** (the machine is shared — this fix reached the planner portal too), **#412**.

**415. `__PF__` is an owner TOKEN, not a name: the overview engines cannot resolve the assigned PF and must never try (2026-08-18). Cross-repo / Client Overview.** `client_overview_load`'s engines (`overview-map1/-regular/-tax/-pft.ts`, plus the shared `utils/tax-plan-steps.ts`) run **before** `enrichClients` and therefore never hold the `clients` row — `assigned_pf` simply is not available to them. So a step owned by the client's PF sets `owner: PF_OWNER` (the literal `"__PF__"`, exported from `overview-engine.ts`), and **exactly one place resolves it**: `actions/clients/overview.ts`, which maps `next_actions[].owner === PF_OWNER → base.assigned_pf || "PF"` after enrichment. **The failure mode is silent and looks like a feature.** A new engine (or a new branch in an existing one) that writes a plausible literal — `"PF"`, `"Planning Facilitator"`, a hard-coded person — compiles, type-checks, renders, and is simply *wrong for every client whose PF is someone else*, with nothing to distinguish it from a correctly-resolved row. The rules: **emit the token, never a name**, for anything owned by the PF; resolve tokens in `overview.ts` only, so there is one resolution site to audit; and if you add a second token (an allocated planner, a specialist), resolve it in the same pass and give it the same `__NAME__` shape so a grep for `__` finds the whole family. Note the resolver walks `next_actions` **only** — a token leaked into `last_action` or a `warnings` entry would render raw as `__PF__` on screen. Cross-ref **#416**.

**416. `next_actions[]` is the real API and `next_action` is a legacy-compat string — a new consumer that reads the old field silently loses the owner AND every fork (2026-08-18). Cross-repo contract.** `computeTrack` returns both, deliberately, and they are NOT the same answer. `next_action` is the bare positional label, unchanged since 2026-07-09 and kept only so an un-deployed frontend keeps rendering. `next_actions` is `[{label, owner}]` and differs in three ways that matter: it substitutes a step's **`groupLabel`** for its label where one is set (the per-specialist Tax 5/Tax 6 rows and MAP 5 collapse to their PHASE, so a track owing five parallel rows says *"Specialist allocation"* instead of singling out whichever specialist sorts first); it carries the **owner**, possibly still as the `__PF__` token (#415); and it is the ONLY field that can hold **more than one entry**, because `opts.parallelNext` lets an engine describe a genuine fork — two people owed work simultaneously — which a single positional string structurally cannot express. A **closed** track returns `[]` for `next_actions` while `next_action` was already null, so those agree.

**The trap is the direction of the divergence.** Reading `next_action` never throws and never looks broken: you get a real label for a real step. You just get *one* of the two lanes during a fork window, no owner, and the raw per-specialist label instead of the phase — three wrong-but-plausible answers. **New consumers read `next_actions`; treat `next_action` as write-only compatibility and delete it once nothing renders it.** The same shape recurs whenever a payload gains a richer sibling field: the old field must keep working (that is the point) which is exactly why nothing forces anyone to migrate. Cross-ref **#415**, **#339** (both fields are positional).


---

**417. A frontend capability flag that FAILS CLOSED on a missing backend field turns an ordinary two-repo change into a hard backend-before-frontend deploy ORDER, and nothing in either repo says so (2026-08-19, v760).** `vault_tax_list` gained a second flag, `can_manage`, so the client Vault tab could split VIEW (the widened `canViewClientTaxVault` = `isTaxAdmin` OR the client's assigned PF) from WRITE (the unchanged 4-person `isTaxAdmin`, plus in-group planners). `ClientVaultTab.jsx` reads it as `canWrite = !!resp.can_manage`, which is the safe default in every sense except deployment: against a backend that predates the field it is `undefined` → `false`, so **shipping the frontend first silently strips Add / Delete / Share from Jake, Tray, Paul and Tracy on every client vault** — no error, no console warning, just chrome that quietly stops existing. The reverse order is harmless (an old frontend ignores an extra field). **Whenever a new response field gates UI a privileged user already has, state the deploy order in the commit body and deploy the backend first** — the fail-closed default that makes the flag correct is the same thing that makes the ordering load-bearing, and a "frontend is safe to ship early" habit is exactly what breaks it. Inverse case of #167 (there a missing gate entry 403s loudly; here a missing field degrades silently). Cross-ref **#385** (view and manage are genuinely different predicates over the same bucket).

**418. TWO "requested extra meeting" bells exist with DIFFERENT title shapes — `'% requested extra meeting'` and `'% requested extra meeting (tax)'` — and one LIKE pattern cannot serve both; the suffix is what keeps their clear contracts separate (2026-08-19, v760).** MAP 1 (`c14`), advisor and accountant all mint a bell ending in `requested extra meeting`; TAX's (`actions/tax/final-decision.ts:159`) ends in `requested extra meeting (tax)`. A naive `title ILIKE '%requested extra meeting%'` matches BOTH, so a MAP 1 acknowledgement would retire a live tax bell for the same client. `actions/automation/stall-ack.ts` therefore lists each literal as its own `extraTitles` entry, byte-copied from the insert site, and the un-suffixed pattern is END-anchored rather than wrapped. **The suffix is not decoration — it is the discriminator that makes two same-client asks independently clearable**, and it is the concrete instance of #407 (two asks answered at different times need different titles). Two further asymmetries ride along: MAP 1 `c14` and TAX `tax_decision` own their extra-meeting bell outright, but advisor/accountant mint ONE bell serving three steps, so it clears only when the row's `extra_meeting_stage` equals the stall being acked; and the neighbouring `advisor|accountant/extra-meeting.ts` still match on an **un-anchored** `'%onboarding=<id>%'` link (5 matches 51), so anchored and un-anchored matching now coexist in the same feature. Cross-ref **#365** (a bell title is an API), **#411**.

**419. The agent tool shell's working directory PERSISTS between invocations, so any repo-specific command must `cd` explicitly IN THE SAME invocation — this is the mechanism behind #337's "same invocation" rule, not just good hygiene (2026-08-19).** Two repos are open at once (`vfo-edge-functions` + `vfo-react`, each with a worktree under `.claude/worktrees/<branch>`) and the shell keeps whatever directory the last call left it in. A bare `git diff`, `npm run build`, `wc -l docs/...` or — worst — `supabase functions deploy` therefore runs against **whichever repo was last visited**, with no error when the command happens to be valid in both. This session ran a deploy attempt from the react cwd; it failed harmlessly, but the same slip in the other direction is #337's stale-worktree deploy with a clean-looking transcript. **Write every shell call as `cd <absolute path> && <command>`, and never rely on a `cd` from a previous call** — including the freshness check and the deploy it guards, which must be one invocation for this reason. Docs make it worse, not better: `docs/` exists in main AND every worktree (#36), so a doc edit from the wrong cwd lands in a checkout nobody is going to commit.

**420. A token whose REPLACEMENT IS MARKUP cannot ride the escaping loop — and the regex you reach for to carve out its run is LEFTMOST-MATCH, so an optional group lets it swallow the whole part (2026-08-19, v761). ALWAYS-APPLIES (string surgery / templating).** Two lessons from one token, and the second is the expensive one. **First: an escaping substitution loop and an XML-valued token are mutually exclusive.** `generate-presentation.ts` substitutes 21 tokens with `split(token).join(xmlEscape(value))` — correct, and correct *because* it escapes. `{{PLANNER_NOTES}}` comes from a textarea, and in DrawingML a bare `\n` inside `<a:t>` renders as **nothing**: the soft break is the `<a:br/>` ELEMENT, a sibling of `<a:r>`. So a two-line note must become run / break / run, its replacement is markup, and pushing it through the shared loop would print `<a:br/>` on the deck as visible text. **The general rule: the moment a token's value can contain STRUCTURE rather than text, it stops being a member of the value map and becomes its own substitution that rewrites the enclosing ELEMENT — and it must run BEFORE the generic loop**, both so the loop cannot escape what it emits and so the literal it locates by is still present. Watch for the same shape anywhere a user-supplied string meets a markup template: line breaks, bullets, links, bold. **Second, and the part that actually bit: `<a:r>(<a:rPr[^>]*>[\s\S]*?</a:rPr>)?<a:t>\{\{PLANNER_NOTES\}\}</a:t></a:r>` is a TRAP.** It reads as "the run containing the token", and on a hand-made fixture it behaves. On the real part it destroyed ~7 KB of speaker notes. The reason is that regex alternation is **leftmost-match, not shortest-match**: the engine tries `<a:r>` at the FIRST run in the part, and with the `rPr` group optional and its body lazy, `[\s\S]*?` is free to expand across every intervening paragraph until it can reach the token — which it can. Laziness only minimises the match **at a given start position**; it does nothing about *which* start position wins, and the earliest one always does. So the "smallest" match you pictured is not the match you get. **The fix is an INDEX WALK, and it is not a workaround — it is the structurally correct tool:** `indexOf` the exact tail `<a:t>{{PLANNER_NOTES}}</a:t></a:r>`, assert it occurs **exactly once**, then `lastIndexOf("<a:r>", tailAt)` — which by construction cannot start anywhere earlier than the nearest preceding run open, and cannot accidentally match `<a:rPr` because the `>` is part of the needle. Then **verify the shape of what you walked onto** (everything between `<a:r>` and `<a:t>` must be a plain `<a:rPr>` and nothing else) so a template drift fails loudly instead of rebuilding from the wrong run. **The meta-lesson is about how it was caught:** this never reached production because the change was **simulated against the real template bytes** before deploying — the diff showed the notes shrinking, which a fixture-sized test would not have. **When you write string surgery against a large real artifact, run it against that artifact and DIFF THE OUTPUT SIZE**, do not settle for "the assertion passed". Cross-ref **#336** (the generator this lives in), **#409** (raw-bytes surgery on packaged files).

**421. The `/connect-card` page's due-now figure MUST mirror the charge sweep's candidate filter; the schedule EMAIL splits by DATE ONLY and must never promise collection. The two surfaces use different filters ON PURPOSE and their totals can legitimately disagree — "fixing" that disagreement toward the email destroys the only promise the page is allowed to make (2026-08-19, v762). ALWAYS-APPLIES (money / client-facing copy).** `migration_connect_load` used to render `net_invoice / 4` labelled *"per quarterly payment"* for every MAP 1 Quarterly row, which is right only for a client who is current. Deb Moerland (client **267**, `pipeline_map1` **138**, net **5,400**) had `pay1`/`pay2` succeeded and `pay3` (2026-03-15) + `pay4` (2026-06-15) unpaid and past due. `contract-chargescheduled-sweep.ts` charges **every** past-due unpaid installment on its next 03:00 UTC run, so the night after she saved a method she would be charged **$2,700** — and the page had just told her **$1,350**. Understating an imminent real charge on the page that collects the method is the worst direction for this error to run.

**What the page now does.** The MAP 1 Quarterly branch of `connect-load.ts` reproduces the sweep's candidate filter **line for line**, from the same file: `pay1_status === 'succeeded'`, `payment_plan === 'Quarterly'`, `n` in 2..4, `payN_date <= today` (UTC date string compare), and `payN_status` not in `{succeeded, processing, pending, declined, auth_required}`. Any hits become `amount` = their sum, `amount_label` = *"total due now"*, plus new `due_now_rows: [{date, amount}]` + `due_now_count` — **spread in only when non-empty**, so the no-past-due, non-Quarterly and TAX responses stay byte-identical and an un-deployed frontend keeps rendering (the same fail-safe shape as #416/#417, and the reason the old-FE-plus-new-BE interim showed one honest *"total due now"* line rather than breaking).

**Why the email is deliberately different, and why that is not a bug to close.** `utils/migration-schedule.ts` splits the unpaid installments into *"Past-due payments:"* and *"Your upcoming payments:"* on **date alone**, and its heading is worded to make **no claim about collection** — because a date-only set legitimately contains rows the sweep will never auto-collect: an unpaid **`pay1`** (the sweep requires `pay1` succeeded before it touches anything), a **`declined`** or **`auth_required`** installment (terminal there, never retried, so a human must intervene), a **`pending`** one, and every **non-Quarterly** row (the sweep skips them entirely). So the email's *"Total due now"* can exceed the page's, on the same row, at the same moment, with both correct. **The rule: only code that mirrors the sweep may promise automatic collection.** If a future change must make the two agree, it agrees by teaching the EMAIL the sweep's filter — never by widening the page's, and never by adding a collection promise to the email's heading. That promise was in fact in the first draft of this change and was removed in review, which is the whole reason this entry exists: the split *looks* like a collection list, so the copy has to work to not be one.

**Two structural asymmetries that make it easy to mis-audit.** (a) The **page recomputes at click time** and self-corrects the instant a deploy lands (#352 property a — the token is a lookup key, never a snapshot); the **email is a snapshot** built at send time whose figures then age, and the sweep's own reminder/auto-resend ladder (#300) rebuilds it fresh each time, so the same client can hold two emails with different past-due tables and neither is wrong. (b) **Stripe's `mode:'setup'` page shows NO amount at all** — a setup session has no line items by design — so there is no third surface to cross-check against and nothing downstream that corrects a wrong figure before money moves. **Every dollar the client sees in this flow comes from exactly these two files**, which is precisely the trap #352 named; this entry is that trap recurring on the MAP 1 branch after #352 fixed the TAX one. Cross-ref **#352** (one story, two files, nobody makes them agree), **#300** / **#302** (the setup-link ladder + per-client sandbox), **#335** (Payment Continuation is a second write path).

**422. An Edit that writes a raw NUL byte into a source string literal makes the file BINARY to git and ripgrep — it silently drops out of every diff and grep surface while still building and running perfectly (2026-08-20, v763).** A sentinel constant was written as `const ADD_NEW_CATEGORY = '<NUL>add_new'` — the leading byte intended as a space landed as `0x00`. Vite/esbuild/Babel all parsed it without complaint, the production build stayed exit 0, and the feature worked, because a NUL is a legal (if insane) character inside a JS string literal. What broke was every tool that classifies files by content sniffing: `git diff` reported **"Binary files differ"** instead of a hunk, so the change was unreviewable and would have landed as an opaque blob in the PR; and **ripgrep skipped the file entirely and silently** — a content grep for a symbol defined in that very file returned nothing, with no warning and exit 0, which reads exactly like "this symbol does not exist here". **The compounding danger is that all three failure modes are silent and the loudest surface (the build) stays green**, so nothing prompts you to look: the file is simultaneously correct at runtime and invisible to the two tools an agent leans on hardest to verify its own work. **Symptom to recognise: a text file that suddenly reports a binary diff, or a grep that finds nothing in a file you know contains the term.** Diagnose with a byte scan (`[System.IO.File]::ReadAllBytes($f)` → count `-eq 0`), which also gives you the exact offset. Fix by rewriting through .NET with **UTF8Encoding `$false`** (no BOM — `Set-Content`/`Out-File -Encoding utf8` reintroduce one, #hub-environment) since the Edit tool cannot match a NUL in `old_string`; then re-verify all three (zero NULs, a text `git diff`, a grep that finds the symbol) before believing it. Generalises past NUL to any control byte that trips git's binary heuristic. Cross-ref **#134** (a source file that builds is not thereby correct at the tooling layer).

**423. A required-field guard written as `!value` rejects every FALSY-BUT-VALID value — and `0` is a legitimate credit cost, price, quantity or count in most of this system (2026-08-20, v763).** `gc_manage_service` opened with `if (!name || !credit_cost) return 400 "Name and credit cost are required"`. Service id 13, *"Website Exploratory Meeting"*, genuinely costs **0** credits (a free consult), so **every** save on that row 400'd — including edits that never touched the cost, because the guard runs before anything else. It had been latent since the handler was written and only became reachable when a new editor put that row in the routine click path. **The correct shape for a numeric required field is presence-then-range, not truthiness:** reject `null`/`undefined`/`''` explicitly, then `Number.isFinite(n)`, then the range test — because `Number(null)` and `Number('')` are both **`0`**, so a bare `Number()` coercion turns a genuinely missing field into a valid free item, which is the same bug pointing the other way. **The mirror-image trap is on the frontend and is NOT the same fix:** a controlled `<input>` yields a **string**, so `!creditCost` on `'0'` is `false` and the FE guard is already correct — meaning the two halves of the same validation legitimately differ, and "fixing" the FE to match the BE would break it. This session mis-flagged the FE guard as buggy three times in a row by reasoning about the value as a number without checking the input's type; **read the actual type at the boundary before porting a validation fix across the repo split.** Cross-ref **#187** (destructure `{data,error}` — the same family of silent-falsy misreads).

**424. A template recipient list can be WRONG FOR AN ENTIRE PIPELINE, for that pipeline's whole life, with zero errors and nothing to notice — because an unresolved role token is dropped SILENTLY. When you add a recipient token to a template, grep that pipeline's handlers for the token FIRST (2026-08-20, v766).** #324/#413 already say *"to know who receives an email, read the HANDLER"* — that is the **diagnostic** rule, applied when you are auditing one send. This is the **preventive** half, and it is the one that was missing. **Nine of the ten `PARTNERSHIP_FAST_TRACK` templates carry `MEMBER` in `cc_list`** (ids 104–110, 196, 197 — every meeting-confirm, meeting-declined, decision and reminder template in the pipeline). **Not one PFT handler had ever passed a `MEMBER` key in its `resolveTemplateRecipients` ctx**, so `MEMBER` resolved to nothing and was dropped on every send, and **the connected member has never been CC'd on a single PFT email since the pipeline was built.** Nothing surfaced it: `resolveTemplateRecipients` drops unresolvable tokens by design (the safe direction, #413 — do NOT harden it into a throw), the drafts were well-formed, the `to` list was always non-empty so the `rcp.to.length === 0` guard never fired, and the Notification/Email Templates editor renders the token as if it were routing. **The only observable symptom was a human eventually noticing an absence** — which is not a symptom you can grep for, and is why this class survives indefinitely.

**Why the whole-pipeline scale is the actual lesson.** A one-template gap gets caught the first time someone reads that draft. A gap that covers *every* template in a pipeline produces a completely self-consistent world: every draft looks the same, so there is no odd one out, and the pipeline's own documentation (the template rows) unanimously asserts the behaviour that is not happening. **Consistency across a pipeline is evidence of a shared code path, NOT evidence that the code path is correct** — when every row agrees, suspect the resolver, not the rows. The fix here was one helper (`memberEmailForClient` in `actions/pft/_shared.ts`: `clients.member_number` → `members.email`, **errors degrade to `null`** so a lookup failure can never break a send) plus **`MEMBER: await memberEmailForClient(...)`** at the four ctx-building call sites that cover all nine templates — `decision-email.ts` (shared ctx), `meeting-email.ts`, and `sweep.ts`'s `sendDiscoveryReminder` + `sendFtReminder`. **The tenth template, `PFT_decision_undecided` (id 193), was deliberately left alone: it carries no `MEMBER` token at all** (empty `to_list`, `cc_list` = two literal staff addresses), so `undecided-email.ts` / `sendUndecidedReminder` needed no change — the audit's answer for a template can legitimately be "nothing to do", and recording *why* is what stops the next reader from "completing" the pattern.

**The rules.** (1) **Adding a role token to a template is a TWO-REPO edit, not a data edit** — the token is inert until a handler passes it, so the template change and the ctx change belong in the same session. (2) **When you touch any template in a pipeline, audit the whole pipeline's token set in one pass** — `select template_name, to_list, cc_list from email_templates where pipeline='X'` beside a grep of `actions/<pipeline>/` for each distinct token; the query is cheap and the gap it finds is unbounded in age. (3) **A token appearing in a template row is a REQUEST, and the handler is the only place it is granted** — treat an unmatched token as a live defect even when nobody has complained, because "nobody has complained" is the expected state. Cross-ref **#324** / **#413** (the diagnostic half), **#325** (these are all Draft-mode rows, so the miss was invisible in Sent too).

**425. One human with TWO portal identities on the SAME email address resolves to ONE role — the wrong one — because `admin_sessions` never records WHICH login minted the token, and `middleware/auth.ts` re-derives the role from the EMAIL alone with a fixed precedence (2026-08-20, investigated — FIXED the same day, v772; see the resolution at the end).** Carson Grover, `cgrover@wealthig.com`, is **tax planner id 3** *and* **member 58014** (his member login was created 2026-08-20 18:41Z). He signed into the MEMBER portal, got a valid session, and every member tab rendered **blank** — Growth Plan showed *"Coming soon"* even though his member category is `advisor` and the data exists. **The session was never wrong; the role lookup was.** `admin_sessions` stores the token, the email and an expiry, and **no `login_type`**, so `middleware/auth.ts` reconstructs the caller's role at request time by probing the login tables in a fixed precedence — **admin → client → specialist → tax_planner → member** — and returning on the first hit. `tax_planner` precedes `member`, so his member session resolved as `callerRole = "tax_planner"`, the deny-by-default gate matched his member-portal actions against `TAX_PLANNER_ALLOWED_ACTIONS`, and every one of them **403'd**. `function_edge_logs` shows the signature plainly: bursts of **three 403s** at 18:41:44, 18:42, 18:43, 18:44 and 18:45 — one burst per tab he clicked.

**Why this is worth a number rather than a bug ticket.** (a) **The precedence list is invisible from both ends** — the member portal has no idea a tax-planner row exists, and the tax-planner portal has no idea a member row was just created; the collision is created by an admin action in a *third* screen. (b) **It is silent and total, not partial** — the caller is authenticated, the token is valid, the session does not expire, and nothing logs "role mismatch"; the portal simply renders empty shells, which reads as "the data isn't there yet" rather than "you are being denied". (c) **It gets worse as the system grows**: every new portal that shares an email namespace adds another way to shadow a lower-precedence identity, and the six login types already overlap by design (staff who are also members, planners who are also advisors). (d) **The reverse case is equally live** — creating a *tax planner* on an email that already has a member login silently promotes that person's existing member sessions, which is an authorization widening, not just a blank screen. **The rule until this is fixed: an email address may hold at most ONE portal identity, and before creating any login row, check the other five tables for that address.** The queued fix is a `login_type` column on `admin_sessions`, stamped by all six login actions and read by `middleware/auth.ts` in place of the email probe (with the probe kept as the fallback for pre-existing sessions) — **flagged, not built.** Cross-ref **#144** (a new login type must join `LOGIN_ACTIONS`), **#257** (the planner allowlist is the whole boundary for a planner caller — which is exactly what he was mistakenly treated as), **#426** (why the symptom was a blank page and not an error).

**FIXED 2026-08-20, `vfo-admin-api` v772 — the queued fix above was built and shipped the same day.** `admin_sessions` gained a nullable `login_type text` (CHECK `admin|member|client|specialist|tax_planner`, migration `20260820210000_add_login_type_to_admin_sessions.sql`), stamped by all six login actions (legacy `login` stamps `'admin'`); `middleware/auth.ts` now resolves the role FROM `login_type` and **re-verifies the identity row still exists in that login table — row gone ⇒ 401, never a fall-through to another role.** Two hardenings rode along: a non-admin-typed session gets `allowedTabs = []` even when the email also holds an `allowed_admins` row, and `isSuperadmin` requires `callerRole === "admin"` as well as the email match. `utils/tax-planner-ownership.ts` learned the same rule via a shared `isPlannerSession()`. **The email-precedence chain survives ONLY as the fallback for legacy NULL-`login_type` sessions** (8h expiry, so it is retirable later) — which means the trap above is still exactly live for any session minted before the deploy, and the lesson stands unchanged for any future role that is inferred rather than recorded. **The "one email = one portal identity" rule is retired: dual identities are supported and each session is locked to the portal that minted it.** No allowlist changed. Verified live on Carson in both directions (member-typed → member portal 200s, planner-typed → planner portal works, cross-portal probes 403). **The rule that replaces it: every new login action MUST stamp `login_type`** — an unstamped session silently drops back onto the legacy probe (pairs with #144).

**426. `src/lib/api.js` redirects to login on a 401 but lets a 403 fail SILENTLY — so every authorization regression in this portal presents as a blank tab, never as an error (2026-08-20).** The shared fetch wrapper treats an expired/invalid session (401) as a routing event and bounces the user to the login page, which is correct and visible. A **403** — the deny-by-default role gate refusing an action the caller is authenticated for — takes no such path: the rejected promise is swallowed by the caller's own `try`/`catch`-to-empty-state, and the component renders its zero-rows view. **The two failures a developer most needs to tell apart ("you're logged out" vs "you're logged in as the wrong role") therefore look completely different from each other and only one of them says anything.** #425 is the worked example: three 403s per tab click, five minutes of them in `function_edge_logs`, and on screen nothing but empty panels and a *"Coming soon"* placeholder — the user's reasonable read was "this feature isn't built", which is the single most expensive wrong conclusion available. **When a portal surface is blank and the data provably exists, check `function_edge_logs` for 403s BEFORE looking at the query, the fixture or the feature flag.** Any fix should surface 403 distinctly (a role-mismatch banner, not a login redirect — redirecting would loop, since the session is valid). Cross-ref **#425**, **#167** (a tab-gated action missing from `TAB_ACTIONS` 403s the same silent way), **#187** (the same family: an error made indistinguishable from "no rows").

**Partly addressed 2026-08-20 (frontend commits `97ad5a5` + `8ce96a2`) — at the PRESENTATION layer only, and the distinction matters.** `src/lib/api.js` is **unchanged**: it still redirects on 401 and still only throws on a 403, so any caller that catches-to-empty-state keeps hiding the denial. What changed is that the four portal shells that were catching silently — Member, Admin, Client and Specialist — now render a visible *"We couldn't load your portal"* banner when their startup load fails (the tax-planner portal already had one), so all five surfaces show **something** instead of empty panels. **The trap therefore still exists anywhere a component swallows a 403 on its own**, and the diagnostic rule is unchanged: **when a portal surface is blank and the data provably exists, read `function_edge_logs` for 403s BEFORE the query, the fixture or the feature flag** — the banner just means you are now more likely to be told to look.

**427. A handler that MUTATES a row it already holds must RE-READ it before any downstream consumer keyed on the mutated column — the stale in-memory object silently filters to zero rows (2026-08-21, `vfo-admin-api` v775).** The membership `checkout.session.completed` branch loads `member_payment_plans` once, then calls `activateMembershipPlan`, which **rewrites `start_date` to the pay date** and writes the year's `member_payment_schedule` rows under that new date. The branch then passed its **pre-activation** object into `draftMembershipConfirmationEmail`, which counts the year with `.eq("year_start", plan.start_date)`. The stale date matched **zero** ledger rows, so `yearMonths = 0`, and a transfer plan's ordinal came out as `firstOrdinal = prior + 1` over `ordinalTotal = prior + 0` — the email read **"Payment 2 of 1"** (Manos 58114, plan 49). The fix is caller-side: re-read by id after activation, pass the fresh row, fall back to the stale one if the re-read fails.

**Why this shape is worth a number.** (a) **Nothing errors.** A filter that matches zero rows is indistinguishable from a legitimately empty ledger, so the only symptom is a wrong number in prose that a human has to notice — the same family as **#187**, one level up: not "an error read as no rows" but "a stale key read as no rows". (b) **The mutation and the consumer are far apart and look unrelated** — the writer is a shared activation util, the reader is an email helper two files away, and the coupling is a *value*, not a call. Neither file is wrong in isolation. (c) **The trigger is a timing coincidence**, which is why it survived so long: `start_date` only diverges when the plan is created on one UTC day and paid on another, so most sign-ups render correctly. (d) **It is narrow but recurring by construction** — any handler that holds a row across a mutating helper has it. **The rule: after calling anything that updates a row you are holding, treat your copy as stale; re-read before passing it on, or pass the id and let the consumer read.** Prefer the caller-side re-read over "fix the filter" — here the `year_start` filter is the correct #316 row-money derivation and changing it would break the money math to paper over a staleness bug. Cross-ref **#187**, **#316**, **#327** (the other "the row already says it happened" family).

**428. Flipping a template to `send_mode=true` without auditing the FE copy that reports it turns every confused re-click into a REAL duplicate send (2026-08-21, `vfo-admin-api` v774).** `member_connect_setup` (id 159) was flipped to auto-send on 2026-08-03. The two "Set Up Payment Details" / "Resend setup email" buttons in `MembersPanel.jsx` kept their **draft-era** success copy — *"Account … created — send the draft from Gmail"* — so the admin, told to go send something, found nothing to send in Drafts and clicked again. `member_stripe_connect_request` had **no re-send guard**, so the second click mailed the member for real: Ade 30007 was emailed twice, three minutes apart. A third defect compounded it — the Settings card branched on the **stale `member.stripe_account_id` prop from `allMembers`**, which the Profile card's send deliberately does not refresh (#245), so after a send the card still showed the first-time CTA rather than "Resend".

**The rules.** (1) **A `send_mode` flip is a UI edit, not a data edit** — in the same pass, grep the frontend for every success/hint string on every caller of that template and delete the words "draft", "Drafts" and "send it from Gmail"; copy that describes a draft is an instruction to double-send. (2) **Any real-sending admin button needs an idempotency stamp** — a nullable `*_email_sent_at` on the subject row, a refusal response the FE can recognise (`{already_sent_at, to_email}` here), and an explicit `force` to override. Guarding the *stamp* is not enough; the guard must sit **before** every side effect, or a refusal still creates the Stripe account and mints the token. (3) **A card that decides "first time vs resend" must ask the server, not a cached list prop** — `member_connect_status` exists precisely because `stripe_account_id`'s presence is a false-green (#318), and a prop from a list load is stale the moment the action succeeds. Cross-ref **#325** (only eleven rows auto-send — editing one reaches a real person), **#356** (mode is keyed in two places per consumer), **#318**, **#245**. **Not yet done: the three sibling connect-request actions (specialist / strategic / tax-planner) are all `send_mode=true` and all still unguarded** — only their copy was corrected.

**429. A NULL in an optional column can BE the switch that selects the old money behaviour — and if a second writer can clear it, the plan silently reverts (2026-08-21, `vfo-admin-api` v776).** Monthly transfer membership plans gained an admin-entered `charge_day` (1–15, the day the member has always paid on). `transferDaySchedule()` returns **null** for any plan that is not a monthly transfer carrying a valid 1–15 day, and **that null is the entire legacy switch**: every one of the five surfaces (`setup-load`, `setup-checkout`, `activate`, `send-setup-link`, and the FE preview) branches on it, so a plan with no day keeps the old *pay-at-the-link* behaviour — `transferLinkPulls`, a first payment collected at the link, the old template — byte for byte. That fallback is deliberate and correct (the 12 pending transfers saved before the column existed had to stay editable). **The trap is that nothing announces which mode a plan is in.** A backfill that misses a row, a form that omits the key, or an edit that clears it produces a plan that looks identical in the list, mints a working link, and quietly charges money at that link instead of scheduling it — the opposite of what the admin intended, with no error anywhere.

**Compounding it: `charge_day` now has TWO writers.** It was an activation-owned column (`activate.ts` derived it from the pay date and stamped it at first payment); `plan-save.ts` now also writes it, **unconditionally**, as `isTransfer && frequency === 'monthly' ? chargeDay : null`. The unconditional write is what keeps an edit *away* from monthly/transfer from stranding a stale day (the same discipline `remaining_payments` already uses), but it means **a plan edit is a live rewrite of a money-routing column**, and `activate.ts` must defer to it rather than re-derive — which is exactly what `chargeDayOut` does (`annual → 15`, `daySched → the admin day`, else the legacy `chargeDayFor(basisDate)`). **The rules.** (1) When a nullable column selects between two money behaviours, say so at the null site and at every consumer — a `?? legacy` that reads like a defensive default is not documentation. (2) After adding such a column, **prove the backfill covers every live row with a query, not a spot-check** (`select id, charge_day from member_payment_plans where transfer and frequency='monthly'` — all 15 rows non-null on 2026-08-21). (3) A column with two writers needs one stated owner per plan state; here plan-save owns it while `setup_pending` and activation only fills the legacy gap. Cross-ref **#349** (the same helper family is basis-date dependent), **#316**, **#414** (a migrated row whose branch column was never backfilled points the whole machine at the wrong branch).

**430. Leaving a pipeline's sandbox toggle ON breaks every LIVE public token link in that pipeline for as long as it is on — and the member sees only "this link is out of date" (2026-08-21).** The membership mode guards (v619) make every `/membership-pay` handler 400 a link whose `plan.sandbox` disagrees with the pipeline's current mode, which is right: a sandbox-stamped plan must never charge a live card. But the guard reads the **pipeline toggle**, so flipping `MEMBER_MEMBERSHIP` to sandbox for a test run **inverts the comparison for every real plan at once** — all of them are `sandbox=false`, so all of their links start 400ing. During this session's sandbox e2e a real member (Gary Watts, plan 39) opened his freshly-emailed setup link and got *"link is out of date — ask VFO Services for a new one"*; nothing was broken and nothing needed re-issuing, the toggle simply had to go back. **The blast radius is every outstanding link in the pipeline, not just the plan under test**, and it is completely silent on the admin side — no bell, no log line anyone reads, and the badge that says SANDBOX is on a panel the affected member cannot see. **The rules.** (1) A sandbox toggle is a **same-sitting borrow**: flip it back the moment the test ends, in the same session, and verify with `select pipeline, sandbox_mode from pipeline_sandbox_config` rather than memory. (2) **Never leave it on across a link-emailing action** — sending setup links and running a sandbox test are mutually exclusive activities in the same pipeline. (3) When a member reports a dead link, **check the pipeline toggle before the token, the expiry or the plan** — the token and the plan are usually fine. (4) The membership toggle is the one that matters most here because `constants/test-sandbox.ts`'s force-sandbox override for test member **59524 does NOT cover membership** (it is member-keyed, not client-keyed, see #251), so membership testing has no per-record alternative to the global toggle. Cross-ref **#251**, **#216**, **#333**.

**431. A status vocabulary written in a migration comment is a SNAPSHOT, not a constraint — enumerate it from the writers before you gate a destructive action on it (2026-08-21, `vfo-admin-api` v777).** `specialist_revenue_requests.payment_status` is a bare `text not null default 'requested'` with **zero CHECK constraints** (verified against `pg_constraint`), and its migration comment reads `-- requested | processing | received | failed`. The live vocabulary is **six** values: `requested`, `pending`, `awaiting_verification`, `processing`, `received`, `failed` — `pending` arrived with the 2026-07-07 house-account model and `awaiting_verification` with the hand-keyed-bank-account path, and neither edited the comment. Building the new `specialist_revenue_delete_request` guard from that comment would have produced an allowlist of "anything not processing/received/failed", which **silently includes `awaiting_verification`** — a row with a live PaymentIntent open on it, hard-deleted. **The rules.** (1) When a guard turns on a status, derive the value set by grepping every writer of that column (`.update({ payment_status:`), not from a comment, a type alias or a doc table — and say in the handler header which six you found, because the next reader has the same problem. (2) Prefer a **positive** allowlist (`!== 'requested'` → refuse) over a negative denylist; a denylist is wrong the moment a seventh value lands, an allowlist merely gets conservative. (3) Back the status test with a **column** test that is true of the thing you actually care about — here `stripe_payment_intent_id || payment_method_type` non-null means money was entered regardless of what the status string says — so a new status value cannot by itself open a destructive path. (4) A stale enum comment in a migration is not worth a migration to fix, but the CURRENT vocabulary belongs in the handler header of anything that branches on it. Cross-ref **#187**, **#305**, **#377**.

**432. The Specialist Revenue payout engine's sandbox mode is the GLOBAL `"MAP 1"` toggle, not a per-client one — so #251's test-member force does NOT cover it, and firing it on the wrong line REAL-SENDS an auto-send email (2026-08-24, `vfo-admin-api` v778).** `utils/specialist-revenue-payout.ts` resolves Stripe mode from the shared `pipeline_sandbox_config` row for **`"MAP 1"`**, because SpecRev lines carry a specialist and a member and often **no client at all** — there is no client to key a per-record override on. `constants/test-sandbox.ts`'s force-sandbox override for test member **59524** is `loadSandboxConfigForClient`-based and **client-keyed** (#251), so it is structurally incapable of covering this engine: testing SpecRev means the global toggle is the only lever, and while it is live, everything the engine touches is live. **The email trap is the sharp edge.** `SPECREV_connect_setup` is one of the eleven `send_mode=true` rows (#325). Firing the engine on a member line whose account **exists but is transfers-inactive** takes the `!transfersActive` branch, which mints a fresh onboarding link and **REAL-SENDS that email to the member** — no draft, nobody in between. The only member path that is email-safe to exercise is the **member-with-NO-account** path, which since v778 mints nothing and emails nothing (it parks `awaiting_connect` + raises the `SPECREV_member_share_held` bell). **The rules.** (1) Before firing `specialist_revenue_payout` on a test row, read `select pipeline, sandbox_mode from pipeline_sandbox_config where pipeline='MAP 1'` — do not assume the test-member override applies. (2) Pick the account state deliberately: no account = safe, half-built account = a real email to a real person. (3) A "test member" force that is keyed on a **client** protects nothing in an engine that has no client — check what the override joins on, not what it is called. Cross-ref **#251**, **#325**, **#430** (the same toggle's blast radius in the other direction), **#159/#317** (transfers-capability checks).

**433. Adding a NON-TERMINAL status means teaching EVERY enumerating predicate about it by hand — engine, every sweep pickup, and every frontend status set — or the leg strands invisibly (2026-08-24, `vfo-admin-api` v778).** The member suspended/paused hold added `"Held - Member Suspended"` / `"Held - Member Paused"` to the `rev_paid` TEXT columns and `held_member_suspended` / `held_member_paused` to `specialist_revenue_lines.payout_status`. These columns are bare `text` with no CHECK (#431 family), so **nothing tells you where the enumerations are** — they are scattered `isResolved` tests, `isRetry` lists, `.in(...)` sweep candidate queries and frontend `PAYABLE`/`PENDING` sets, each written as a closed list by someone who knew the vocabulary at the time. This change had to touch **four different kinds of them**: MAP 1's unresolved test (which happened to admit the new values already), tax's `isRetry` (which did not, and would have stranded every held tax leg forever), SpecRev's sweep candidate query (fixed by **exporting `PAYABLE_STATUSES` from the engine and having the sweep import it**, so the two can never drift again), and a **new held-only PIP pass** because the existing pass's predicate could not be widened without also picking up unrelated `"Pending"` failures. On the frontend the same list appears again in `OPEN_PAYOUT`, `PENDING_PAYOUT` and both `PricingSplitCard` case statements; a miss there does not strand money but **mis-renders it as settled or missing**. **The rules.** (1) A new non-terminal status is a **grep-the-vocabulary change**, not a one-file change: search both repos for every existing status literal in that column before writing the first branch. (2) Prefer **exporting the list from the engine and importing it into the sweep** over restating it — a shared constant converts a silent drift into a compile error. (3) When an existing pass cannot be widened without changing unrelated behaviour, **add a new pass** rather than loosening the old predicate. (4) A held/parked status is only safe if **every** retry path accepts it (#303) — enumerate those paths and say in the header which ones you checked. Cross-ref **#303**, **#377**, **#380**, **#431**.

**434. `member_profile_save` is a blind spread upsert AND several call sites save PARTIAL profiles, so any transition detection must RE-READ the row after the write — never diff against the payload (2026-08-24, `vfo-admin-api` v778).** The instant release of held revenue-share legs fires when a member transitions *out* of suspended/paused, which means the handler has to know both the before and the after. Neither is available the obvious way. The handler spreads whatever keys the caller sent straight into the upsert, and **several frontend call sites send a subset of the profile** — a payload with no `paused` key is not "paused = false", it is "paused unchanged", and merging the payload over the prior row to predict the result gets that backwards for every partial save. The implementation therefore does **two** reads: the prior row **before** the upsert, and a fresh read of the same row **after** it, comparing `memberHoldReason()` across the pair. The post-write read is not defensive politeness — it is the only place the true new state exists, because the upsert's own return is not what a partial payload plus the DB's existing values compose to in the caller's head. **The rules.** (1) For any "did this field just change" logic on a spread upsert, the after-state comes from a **re-read**, never from the payload and never from a merge you computed. (2) Before adding transition detection to a save handler, check whether its callers can send partial payloads — if any can, payload-diffing is wrong for all of them. (3) State the two read points in the handler header; the next reader will otherwise "optimise away" the second one as a duplicate query. This is **#427** one level up: #427 is a stale row *after a helper mutated it*, this is a stale row *you never had in the first place*. Cross-ref **#427**, **#187**, **#240**.

**435. A one-time Checkout line item combined with a FUTURE `billing_cycle_anchor` is invoiced at the ANCHOR, not at completion — the immediate-charge construct is `subscription_data[trial_end]` (2026-08-24, `vfo-admin-api` v780).** The Specialist License Fee Continuation catch-up branch has to collect the current month AT SETUP and then bill monthly from next month's charge day. The obvious shape — a recurring line plus a one-time $99 line, with `billing_cycle_anchor` pushed to next month and `proration_behavior=none` — silently defers the one-time item too: Stripe holds it and puts it on the **anchor's** invoice, so the money the admin believed was collected today arrives a month late, on the same invoice as the first recurring charge. Nothing errors and the Checkout session completes normally; the only symptom is a first invoice for $198 thirty days later. **The working construct is the "setup fee + trial" pattern:** keep both line items, drop the anchor, and set `subscription_data[trial_end]` to the target date instead. Under a trial, Checkout invoices one-time line items **immediately at completion** and the recurring price starts at `trial_end` — which is the behaviour the deferred branch gets from an anchor, so the two branches end up structurally different for the same-looking outcome. **The cost is wording you cannot remove:** Stripe's hosted page renders its own trial/free language ("no payment due today" and similar) even though a $99 one-time item IS being collected on that page. It is Stripe-controlled, not template text; `custom_text[submit][message]` is the only lever, and the deliberate decision here was to accept the mismatch rather than lose the immediate collection. **The rules.** (1) When a subscription setup must move money TODAY and also start a schedule LATER, reach for `trial_end`, not an anchor — an anchor defers everything on the session, one-time items included. (2) `proration_behavior=none` is about the recurring price only; it says nothing about one-time line items and will not rescue them. (3) Verify the shape by reading the **first invoice's** amount and date in Stripe, not by the session completing — both branches complete identically. (4) If the hosted page's wording matters more than the timing, the alternative is a separate one-time PaymentIntent, which costs a second mandate. Cross-ref **#298** (the same SpecRev ACH setup this mirrors), **#287**, **#315**.

**436. A guard documented in the architecture docs is NOT thereby implemented — grep for the code before relying on a doc's assertion that a handler is protected (2026-08-24, `vfo-admin-api` v780).** [architecture/07-server-chains.md](architecture/07-server-chains.md) had carried, for two months, the line *"Must skip the $0 `subscription_create` stub (**#198**)"* under the specialist licence invoice chain. **`processSpecialistLicenseInvoicePaid` had no such check.** #198 was written up when the guard was added to the SpecRev processor; the licence processor was then described as if it had inherited it, and never did. The exposure was real and **pre-existing on the onboarding path**, not just the new continuation one: a $0 `subscription_create` stub invoice would have claimed `lic_last_invoice_id`, minted a bogus $99 invoice + receipt PDF pair to Drive, drafted the invoice/receipt email and written a $99 ledger row for money nobody paid — and, because the claim is per-invoice idempotent, the REAL first invoice would then have been skipped as already-processed. It survived because live onboarding subscriptions billed immediately and never emitted the stub; the deferred continuation branch is the first shape that does. The guard now exists (`amount_due`/`total` must be > 0, mirroring SpecRev). **The rules.** (1) A doc sentence asserting that code checks something is a **claim about code**, and the handler is the authority (**#399** family) — when a doc says "must skip X", grep for the skip before building anything on top of it, and treat its absence as a live bug rather than a doc typo. (2) Docs written when a pattern is *established* tend to describe the pattern, not the per-call-site rollout; when one processor gains a guard, name in the doc **which** processors have it, never the family. (3) A guard whose absence is invisible because the triggering shape never occurs is not "unused" — it is untested, and the first new branch that produces the shape inherits the whole bug. (4) When you add the missing guard, say in the CHANGELOG that it fixes a **pre-existing latent** bug on the old path too, or the next reader will scope the fix to the new feature. Cross-ref **#198**, **#399**, **#376** (git/code wins over prose), **#327**.

**437. `scheduled` joined the `lic_payment_status` vocabulary — a subscription that exists with zero money moved (2026-08-24, `vfo-admin-api` v780).** A DEFERRED License Fee Continuation setup completes Checkout with only a mandate captured: `lic_subscription_id` is written, nothing is charged, and the first charge is a calendar event weeks away. Recording that as `processing` (the old ACH value) would claim a payment that does not exist, and a `payment_made` progress marker would lie on the tracker — so the state is **`lic_payment_status='scheduled'`** with progress `task_key='payment_setup'`. Like every status column here it is bare `text` with no CHECK (**#431**), so **nothing tells you where the readers are**: the value must be taught by hand to the sweep's "done" test, the outstanding-links state mapper, the FE chip sets, and anything deciding whether a licence row is settled. Two consequences worth stating separately. (a) **"Set up" and "paid" are now different questions on the same row.** Sweep tier 7b's completion test is `lic_subscription_id IS NOT NULL`, deliberately NOT a payment test — a payment-based predicate would chase a specialist who already finished. Anything else asking "is this done?" must pick the same axis on purpose. (b) **The double-enroll guard cannot key on payment status either, and the natural PostgREST spelling is wrong:** `.neq("lic_payment_status","canceled")` **drops NULL rows**, which is exactly the deferred subscription you are trying to catch — filter on the subscription id in SQL and do the not-canceled test in JS. The pending list deliberately **passes unknown status values through raw** (`past_due` and friends) rather than coercing them, so a future Stripe value renders visibly instead of silently reading as settled. Cross-ref **#431**, **#433** (adding a non-terminal status means teaching every enumerating predicate), **#303**, **#377**.

**438. A JS `String.replace` REPLACEMENT STRING eats `$1` / `$&` as a capture reference, so any money-bearing token substitution silently corrupts itself (2026-08-25, `vfo-admin-api` v782→v787). ALWAYS-APPLIES (money, email).** `body.replace(/\[FINAL_RETAINER\]/g, "$12,500.00")` does not insert `$12,500.00` — the `$1` is read as "capture group 1", and with no group 1 in the pattern the engine emits the literal `$1` followed by `2,500.00`, or worse, silently substitutes a group that DOES exist. It bit this branch **twice**: the final-retainer sentence inserted into the Client decision 1 body, and again in the confirmation email's `[Payment Amount]`. The failure is invisible in code review because the string looks right, and invisible in testing unless the test amount happens to be one whose corruption is legible. **The rules.** (1) **Every substitution whose replacement carries a user- or DB-supplied value uses a replacer FUNCTION** — `.replace(/\[TOKEN\]/g, () => value)` — never a string, and this is cheap enough to apply unconditionally rather than case by case. (2) Money is the loudest case but not the only one: any value that may contain `$` qualifies (a name, a note, a refund reason). The precedent is `automation_TAX_depositrefund`'s `[Refund Reason]`, which already did this and documented why in `architecture/05-api-action-catalog.md`. (3) The multi-token `subst()` helper every tax handler builds is the right place to enforce it — convert the whole chain at once, because one string replacement among twenty functions is exactly what survives review. Cross-ref **#324**/**#413** (tokens the handler must actually pass), **#356**, **#187**.

**439. `agreement_templates.field_map` page numbers are coupled to CONTENT LENGTH — adding one paragraph to an agreement body silently moves the signature block to the next page and BoldSign then places signatures on blank space (2026-08-25, `vfo-admin-api` v782→v787).** The revised fee process added an addendum paragraph to agreement rows 8 and 20. That paragraph pushed the pre-page-break content from 2 pages to 3, so the hard page break landed the signature block on **page 4 instead of page 3** — and `field_map`'s five signature fields still said `p:3`. Had the body UPDATE shipped without the page bump in the same statement, every live BoldSign send off those rows would have placed the client and CEO signature fields on the wrong page, on documents real people sign. **The rules.** (1) **A body edit and its `field_map` page fix are ONE statement**, never two migrations and never two sessions — there is no intermediate state that is safe to leave live. (2) Coordinates are not guessable from the HTML: source them by **placing the fields visually in a BoldSign template and reading them back**, which is what the throwaway `boldsign-template-fields` edge function exists for (deploy it, read, delete it). (3) X/Y survive a pagination shift when everything after the page break is unchanged — only the page index moves — but prove that per row rather than assuming it. (4) The authoritative page check is the **first sandbox send's real render** (BoldSign uses html2pdf.app), not a local Chromium render; use the local render to predict and the sandbox send to confirm. (5) `agreement_templates.boldsign_template_id` was a read-but-unused column for years (`integrations/boldsign.md` says so); it is now meaningfully stamped for exactly this workflow, so do not "clean it up". Cross-ref **#79**, **#88**, **#342** (an external asset read by name is coupled to the deploy), **#410**.

**440. `isThreePaymentPlan` is keyed on `final_retainer_amount`, NOT `initial_retainer_amount` — a predicate keyed on initial silently misclassifies every plan that was converted back to 2 payments (2026-08-25, `vfo-admin-api` v786).** On the revised tax fee process a total above $30,000 splits the retainer into an initial ($15,000 flat) and a final payment. A Tax 4 amendment down to $30,000 or less **converts the plan back to 2 payments by nulling `final_retainer_amount` while deliberately KEEPING `initial_retainer_amount`** — the kept value is both the record that $15,000 was collected as an initial retainer and the marker `amend-fee.ts` uses to convert *back* if a later amendment crosses the threshold again (reversible until Client decision 1 goes out). Both columns are always written together at pricing, so on every non-converted row the two tests are identical and a wrong predicate passes all normal testing. **The rules.** (1) When a conversion nulls one of a pair of columns and keeps the other as a marker, **the nulled column is the predicate** — write down which one and why, at the constant. (2) The predicate has THREE copies that must move in lockstep: the edge constant (`constants/tax-fee-process.ts`) and both frontend mirrors — same class of cross-repo coupling as the step machine (**#339**). (3) `retainer_amount` stays the FULL 50% retainer in both shapes; only *how* it is collected changes, which is what lets every existing invoice/receipt/revshare calculation keep working untouched. Any new code reading `retainer_amount` as "one payment that moved" is wrong on a 3-payment plan — that was the real bug behind the payments-tab display and the webhook's card-fee derivation, which computed a large NEGATIVE fee from the full retainer. Cross-ref **#339**, **#429** (a NULL can BE the switch selecting a money behaviour), **#414**, **#252**.

**441. A DEFERRED payout is held only by the gate you can name — the sweep may already be picking the row up on a precondition that is satisfied (2026-08-25, `vfo-admin-api` v785).** On a 3-payment tax plan the retainer revenue share does not fire at the client's green click (only half the retainer is collected then); it is deferred to the webhook that confirms the FINAL retainer, where it runs once against the full `retainer_amount`. The mechanism is the legs staying NULL (**#377**) — but `retainer_receipt_number`, which is the tax revshare sweep's *candidate* precondition, **is stamped at the INITIAL payment**, so the sweep sees the plan as a candidate the whole time it is waiting. The only thing preventing an early partial payout is the sweep's retry-only gate (it acts on legs that are `"Pending"`, not on NULL legs). **The rules.** (1) Never pre-stamp a deferred leg with anything — `"Pending"` included — and never "tidy" a NULL leg into a status. (2) When you defer a payout, **state in the code and the doc WHICH gate is holding it**, because "it hasn't fired yet" is not evidence of a guard; a sweep-safety argument that does not name a predicate has not been made. (3) Check whether every OTHER precondition on the deferral path is already satisfied — a deferral built on the assumption that "the receipt number isn't there yet" is a bug waiting for the first plan whose receipt lands early. Cross-ref **#377**, **#380**, **#362**, **#398** (the implementation charge's single-call-site rule, the same "nothing may chain this from a sweep" principle), **#303**.

**442. A dropdown that COMMITS on select turns a mis-click into a money decision — a money step's dropdown must confirm first (2026-08-25, `vfo-admin-api` v786 + FE).** The house convention for a step dropdown is that choosing an option completes the step immediately. Applied to the Tax 4 / Tax 5 "Amend fee" step, picking "Keep the fee as it is" would have finalised the engagement's price on a stray click, with the Client decision 1 email — which quotes those figures to the client — unlocked immediately after. The step therefore **confirms before committing the keep**, and the answer stays **editable until the downstream decision is recorded**, which is the real backstop: reversibility, not just a dialog. **The rules.** (1) Before reusing a save-on-select control, ask what the selection *does*; if it moves money, changes a price, or unlocks a client-facing send, add the confirm. (2) Pair the confirm with a **re-answerable window** bounded by the first irreversible downstream event, and make the backend enforce that window too — the UI lock alone is choreography (**#353**, **#403**). (3) State the two "done" sources for such a step (its own progress row OR the amendment stamp) so a lost save-task after a successful money write does not leave the step reading outstanding while the money has moved. Cross-ref **#242**, **#249**, **#274**, **#353**, **#403**.

**443. `notification_rules` list columns are jsonb, not `text[]` — an `ARRAY[...]` literal in a seed fails with 42804 (2026-08-25).** `recipients` and `default_recipients` on `notification_rules` follow the same convention `email_templates` already uses for `to_list` / `cc_list` / `bcc_list` (**#53**, **#117**): jsonb, seeded as a JSON array literal (`'["a@b.com"]'::jsonb`), never as a Postgres array constructor. The error message (`42804 column … is of type jsonb but expression is of type text[]`) is clear, but it lands mid-transaction on a multi-statement seed, so the whole batch rolls back and the *other* statements look like they failed too. Check the column type before writing any seed that touches a list column on either table — the two tables are the only places this shape occurs, and both chose jsonb. Cross-ref **#53**, **#117**, **#176**, **#223** (a multi-statement `execute_sql` returns only the LAST result, so a mid-batch failure is easy to misread).

**444. A status DENYLIST in a CHARGE sweep charges every value it has never heard of — invert to a positive charge-only-on-EMPTY test BEFORE introducing any new terminal status (2026-08-26, `vfo-admin-api` v789). ALWAYS-APPLIES (money).** `contract-chargescheduled-sweep.ts` decided what to charge with `const skipStatuses = new Set(["succeeded","processing","pending","declined","auth_required"]); ... if (status && skipStatuses.has(status)) continue;` — *"charge unless the status is one of these five"*. That predicate was correct for as long as it existed, for the unremarkable reason that those five plus NULL were **all the values that existed**; its real meaning had always been *"charge only when nothing has happened yet"*, and nothing forced anyone to notice the difference. The 2026-08-26 "Cancel all remaining payments" button writes a **sixth** value, `pay{N}_status='cancelled'`, and the old predicate would have **charged every cancelled installment on the very next nightly run** — real money, off-session, on saved cards, against exactly the clients VFO had just decided to stop billing. The fix is one line (`if (status) continue;`) and is behaviour-identical for every value the column has ever held, because the only other charge-eligible shape (`check_pending`) never reaches the loop — the select filters `payment_method_type` to card/ach. **The rules.** (1) **A sweep that MOVES MONEY gets a positive allowlist, never a denylist.** An unknown status means *"someone else owns this slot"*, not *"charge it"*, and the asymmetry is the whole argument: a wrong denylist over-charges silently, a wrong allowlist merely under-charges and shows up as a stuck row somebody chases. (2) **Adding a value to a bare-`text` status column is a two-part change** — the writer, plus an audit of every predicate that ENUMERATES that column. Grep for the COLUMN name, not for the new value, which by definition appears nowhere yet (this is **#433**'s rule pointed at sweeps). (3) When you invert such a predicate, keep the old set's members skipped for their *original* reasons and write those reasons at the site, or the next reader will "simplify" `declined`/`auth_required` back into the charge path and start re-raising a card that already failed, nightly. (4) The inversion is **not provable by testing** — the value that breaks it does not exist until you ship it, so this has to be caught by reasoning at review time. Cross-ref **#431** (positive allowlist over denylist, the read-side twin), **#433**/**#437**, **#327**, **#303**.

**445. The `experts` payload is `select("*")` + SUBTRACTIVE per-caller redaction — the exact INVERSE of the members payload's additive whitelist, so a new private column is PUBLIC until you strip it, at all three sites (2026-08-26, `vfo-admin-api` v789). ALWAYS-APPLIES (security).** The members portal payload is built by naming the columns that may leave the server (**#207**): a column added to `members` is private by default, and a forgotten whitelist entry shows up as a *missing field*, which is loud and gets fixed in minutes. `experts` is built the other way — the query is `select("*")` and each non-admin caller then deletes the fields it must not expose. A column added to `experts` is therefore **exposed by default**, and a forgotten strip is a silent leak with no symptom whatsoever: the surface renders correctly, nobody 403s, and the private value simply rides along in the JSON to every specialist, client and member portal that loads the directory. Adding `experts.personal_email` meant editing **three** strip sites — `actions/data/load-data.ts` (the non-admin branch), `actions/data/specialist-showroom-load.ts` and `actions/data/client-showroom-load.ts` — and missing any one of them would have shipped real people's personal addresses to the other two audiences. **The rules.** (1) **Never infer a payload's redaction direction from a sibling payload.** Open the query: `select("*")` means subtractive and you owe a strip at every caller; an explicit column list means additive and you owe nothing. (2) For a subtractive payload the strip sites are a SET, and that set belongs in the table doc beside the column — three today, and a count in prose is exactly the thing that rots (**#402**), so name the FILES, not the number. (3) There is a **second, independent** list: the narrow `anon`/`authenticated` column grant (`20260709160000_experts_anon_column_grants.sql`). A new column does **not** join it automatically — which is the desired default — but it means *"I checked the grant"* and *"I checked the strips"* are two different checks and neither implies the other. (4) The durable fix is to convert the payload to an explicit column list, at which point this entry retires; until then treat **every** `experts` migration as a security change and run the invariant-4 advisor pass on it. Cross-ref **#207**, **#141**, **#142**, **#309**.

**446. Deleting the last WRITER of a token column does not make its readers dead code — the links are already in people's inboxes, and "cleaning up" the route breaks them (2026-08-26, `vfo-admin-api` v789).** Removing the portal-setup button from all four first-payment emails (two `email_templates` bodies by live SQL, two handler render paths in code) left `clients.client_setup_token` with **zero writers system-wide**, while `load_client_setup` / `submit_client_setup` and the public `/client-setup` page still exist and still work. Every static signal says dead code: no writer, no caller minting a token, no link in any template, no frontend entry point. It is not dead — every client ever sent a first-payment email holds a live link to it, resolving against rows already in the table, potentially for years. The state deserves its own word in the docs: **orphaned, not retired.** **The rules.** (1) When you remove a minter, say so **at the reader** and **at the column**, in those words — *"no writer as of \<date\>; readers retained for links already sent"* — or the next tidy-up pass deletes a working route on impeccable reasoning. (2) A "no writers" grep is evidence about the FUTURE only. For anything reachable by a URL that was emailed, the population of live credentials is whatever was minted historically, and **nothing tells you when the last one gets used**. (3) Retiring such a route is a separate, later, **dated** decision with its own mechanism (expire the tokens, or land a redirect to the replacement flow) — never a side effect of removing the feature that minted them. (4) Name the SUCCESSOR in the same breath — here the client's route in is now the manual login-setup email to `/set-password` — because a reader who finds an orphaned route with no successor documented will conclude the capability was lost and rebuild it. Cross-ref **#341** (the same handler's earlier half-removal), **#308**, **#399**.

**447.
