An email carrying a RAW expiring credential is coupled to its own re-send cadence — the link's TTL, not any policy, is what makes a sweep mail the same person every night (2026-08-26, `vfo-admin-api` v789).** `SPECREV_connect_setup` embedded a raw Stripe `account_links` URL, which expires in minutes. Because the link could not be allowed to go stale, the payout engine re-minted and re-sent the whole email on **every** nightly pass over an `awaiting_connect` line — and since `awaiting_connect` is in `PAYABLE_STATUSES`, the line was re-entered forever. A recipient who ignored the mail got it again the next day, and the next, with nobody ever having decided that was the chasing policy. It surfaced as a user complaint only because a second bug (a $0 share falling through to that branch) made the daily mail read *"revenue share payment of $0.00"*; the daily re-send itself had been running unremarked. The fix is two changes that must land together: the button became the **durable `/payout-setup?token=` page** (one token per entity, mints a fresh Stripe onboarding link per click, never expires — **#268**), and the initial send became **once-only**, guarded on a pre-write read of `email_drafted_at` and stamped only on an actual send. Chasing then moved where chasing belongs — paced sweep tiers at 2 and 4 business days, each stamped once (`connect_reminder_sent_at`, `connect_pf_notified_at`) and each backed by a `notification_rules` row. **The rules.** (1) **An email is a durable artifact; a credential with a short TTL does not belong inside one.** Send a durable page that mints the short-lived thing on demand — that indirection is precisely what decouples send-cadence from link-freshness. (2) When you find a handler re-sending on every tick, **look for the expiring payload before assuming a missing dedupe**: the dedupe is often *deliberately* absent because someone reasoned "a stale link is worse than a duplicate email", and adding a guard without fixing the link would have shipped one dead link and silence. (3) **Cadence is a product decision and must be written as a ladder** (stamp + `businessDelayCutoffIso` + a seeded rule), never left as a side effect of a technical constraint — the ladder is also the only version an operator can re-time, re-route or switch off without a deploy (**#176**). (4) A once-only send guard reads its stamp column **before** the write and stamps only on a real send, or the first transient failure silences that recipient permanently. Cross-ref **#268**/**#317**/**#159**, **#396**/**#397**, **#176**, **#325**, **#404**.

**448. A predicate is only as good as the SELECT feeding it — a discriminator column missing from a handler's `.select(...)` makes that predicate silently, permanently FALSE (2026-08-26, `vfo-admin-api` v791). ALWAYS-APPLIES (money, email).** This is the enforcement half of **#440**. `isThreePaymentPlan` is keyed on `final_retainer_amount`; three handlers shipped on 2026-08-25 whose `.select(...)` fetched `fee_process_version` and `initial_retainer_amount` and **omitted the one column the predicate reads**. PostgREST returns the row without the unselected column, so the property is `undefined`, `undefined != null` is FALSE, and the predicate answers "not a 3-payment plan" for every 3-payment plan forever. There is no error, no warning and no missing-field symptom — **because the FALSE branch is the pre-existing, correct-looking legacy behaviour.** The failure mode is therefore *"the new feature silently does not exist"* rather than a crash, which is why all three survived a full end-to-end test pass and were then found on 2026-08-26 by a single user observation about wrong wording in a bell. What they were doing meanwhile: `confirmation-email.ts` told the PF the client had *"paid the $23,019.00 retainer"* instead of the $8,331.50 initial; `payment-email.ts` said "retainer" and promised only "the implementation fee" on real sends, because `[RETAINER_LABEL]` / `[LATER_PAYMENTS]` are live in `email_templates` rows 22 and 131 today and the in-file comments claiming they were "no-op on today's bodies" had gone stale (**#399** — the code is the authority, and here the comment was actively lying); and `paidbycheck.ts` would have asked a check-paying client to write a check for the **full retainer**. **The rules.** (1) When you add a shared predicate to a constants file, **the column(s) it reads become part of its CONTRACT** — write them in a comment at the predicate, in the form *"requires `x` in the caller's select"*, because the predicate's own file cannot enforce anything about a query it never sees. (2) A `select("*")` caller is safe by construction; **every explicit-column caller must be audited at EVERY call site in the same change** that introduces the predicate, not later. There were 21 consumers here and the three that were wrong looked identical to the eighteen that were right. (3) When auditing, **grep for the predicate NAME, not for the column** — a caller that omits the column is exactly the caller whose text does not contain it. (4) The general shape to fear: **a bug that defaults to the OLD behaviour is far harder to see than one that defaults to broken.** Testing does not find it, because the old behaviour is what the system did last week and it still renders, still sends, still books money. It needs a deliberate call-site audit at review time. (5) The same discipline applies to the fix: `implementation_card_fee` was added to `confirmation-email.ts`'s select **in the same edit** that made it read the column. Cross-ref **#440**, **#427** (the other way a handler ends up deciding on a row that does not say what it thinks), **#187**, **#207**/**#445** (an explicit column list is a contract in the payload direction too), **#339**.

**449. A date-less Stripe idempotency key is scoped to a LOGICAL payout, so anything that legitimately changes that payout's AMOUNT within 24 hours collides — and an amount that has NOT changed silently replays the earlier transfer as a fresh success (2026-08-26).** The tax revenue-share transfers key on `revshare-tax-<plan id>-<kind>` and `planner-tax-<plan id>-<kind>`. The absence of a date is deliberate and correct per **#228**: it is what lets a sweep retry a transfer that committed at Stripe but whose result was lost, without paying twice. Stripe stores an idempotency key for **24 hours** and refuses reuse with different parameters. Two things reach that window. Testing the same plan id twice in one day is the obvious one. The production one is not obvious at all: **amend a fee within 24 hours of a FAILED transfer attempt, and the sweep's retry carries a new amount on the old key.** In this session's incident the amounts differed by **exactly one cent** — four back-to-back amendments had drifted the member and planner legs by a cent, with VFOS absorbing the residual per **#394** — and Stripe raised an idempotency error. **That error is the SAFE outcome, and the reason to write this entry is the branch where it does not happen:** had the two amounts matched, Stripe would have returned the EARLIER transfer as a success, the handler would have marked the leg paid, and **no new money would have moved** while every record said it had. **The rules.** (1) **An amendment feature and a logical idempotency key interact**, and that interaction has to be reasoned about when *either* one is added — neither is wrong alone. (2) The collision self-heals: the key ages out after 24 hours and the sweep retries `Failed` legs, so it is a **delay, not a loss** — but only because that retry exists. **Verify the retry path covers the status the collision leaves behind** before relying on this; a collision that parks a leg in a status no sweep enumerates is a permanent loss wearing a temporary error's clothes (**#303**). (3) When re-testing a money flow on a fixture, **use a NEW parent row id rather than resetting the old one** — the key is derived from that id, so a reset row inherits its own history and the second test either errors or lies. That is how this session unblocked itself. (4) **Never treat an idempotency error as noise to be retried around.** It is the system telling you two different amounts claim to be the same payment; the answer is to find out which one is right, not to add a suffix. Cross-ref **#228**, **#15**, **#327**, **#394**, **#377**, **#362**.

**450. A column named for a concept that occurs more than once per record will be silently overwritten by each occurrence — and the tell is a caller passing `0` instead of reading it (2026-08-26, `vfo-admin-api` v792).** `client_tax_plans.card_processing_fee` was written by all three tax charges: retainer, final retainer, implementation. Nothing broke loudly, because each receipt renders at charge time from the just-written value and is then a static PDF — **the loss only shows on a RE-RENDER**, which is exactly what the revised fee process's fresh-invoice paths do. Proven live: an $8,331.50 initial retainer stamped $249.14, then the $11,668.50 final retainer replaced it with $348.80, and the initial payment's fee no longer existed anywhere. The strongest evidence was sitting in the codebase the whole time: two receipt handlers passed `cardProcessingFee: 0` with comments explaining that the column *"holds THIS charge's fee"* and therefore could not be trusted for the invoice's retainer row — **a workaround that documented the defect precisely while normalising it.** The fix adds `final_retainer_card_fee` and `implementation_card_fee`, and both `0`s became the real value. **The rules.** (1) When a one-per-record column becomes N-per-record — a payment split into instalments, a document re-issued, a fee charged per stage — **splitting the column is the fix; a discriminator is not.** A "which payment does this row describe" flag makes every reader conditional and still stores only one of the N values. (2) **Keep the ORIGINAL column's meaning pinned to the FIRST occurrence** and add siblings for the rest, so no legacy writer and no legacy reader has to move — here `card_processing_fee` still means the first retainer payment, `invoice-receipt.ts` was not touched, and the migration needed **no backfill** (production exposure was verified to be zero before that was claimed). (3) Put the ownership in a SQL **`comment on column`** for all N columns including the original, so the split is discoverable from the database rather than only from whichever handler you happen to open. (4) Treat a **"pass 0 / pass null instead"** workaround carrying an explanatory comment as **evidence of a schema problem, not a solved one** — a comment good enough to explain why a real value cannot be read is good enough to be a bug report. (5) The next instance of this exact shape is on the same table and is knowingly unfixed: **`client_tax_plans.payment_method_type` is one shared column across all three payments**, so a re-issued invoice for a plan whose payments used different methods reads the LATEST method and can drop the initial retainer's card-fee disclosure. Cross-ref **#440**, **#429**, **#128**, **#287**, **#402**.

**451. A shared resolver's OPTIONAL column must distinguish `undefined` (the caller's `.select(...)` never carried it → look it up) from `null` (genuinely none) — or every explicit-select caller silently drops the second recipient (2026-08-26, `vfo-admin-api` v793). ALWAYS-APPLIES (bells, email).** The defensive half of **#448**. When `utils/tax-planner-notify.ts notifyAllocatedPlanner` gained a second recipient slot (`plan.tax_team_member_id`), it inherited #448's failure shape exactly: a dozen handlers pass a plan object built from an explicit column list, so any one of them that did not name the new column would hand the resolver `undefined`, the resolver would read that as "no team member", and the bell would reach one person forever — **with no error, no missing-field symptom and no visible difference from the correct pre-change behaviour.** The fix is to make the type `number | null | undefined` and branch on all three: `undefined` triggers a one-query lookup by plan id, `null` skips it, a number is used. **The three-state read is what makes the audit optional rather than load-bearing** — every explicit-select caller was still updated in the same change (eleven of them), but the one that was NOT (`router/webhooks.ts`'s `mintDecision2Bell`, an off-limits file that session) is correct anyway, just one query slower. **Design rule: when you add an optional field to a shared resolver's input type, make "the caller didn't tell me" a distinct, recoverable state from "the caller told me there is none."** A plain `?: T | null` collapses the two and converts every un-audited call site into a silent permanent bug. Pairs with #448 (name a predicate's columns at the predicate) and #324 (a recipient token does nothing unless the handler passes it).

**452. Splitting ONE column into TWO slots silently invalidates every OTHER guard that was reading that column as "somebody is allocated" — and the guards that break are the ones you did not touch (2026-08-26; live in `vfo-admin-api` v793, FIXED the same session in v794). ALWAYS-APPLIES (cross-repo contracts).** `client_tax_plans.tax_planner_id` had carried two different meanings at two different times: the Team Member coordinator holding the plan pre-hand-off, and the Advanced Tax Planner running it afterwards. Splitting them into `tax_planner_id` + `tax_team_member_id` was correct and every site that *meant* "the planner" was updated — but **two untouched guards had been relying on the overload to mean "anybody"**: `actions/tax/ready-for-tax3.ts:72` (`decision !== "declined" && !plan.tax_planner_id` → 400 *"Allocate a Team Member / Tax Planner before sending the confirmation email"*) and `actions/tax/skip-roi-meeting.ts:98`. Their error strings say "Team Member / Tax Planner" — they were written for the overloaded column — so after the split a plan legitimately held by a Team Member alone **cannot send its ROI confirmation email or skip the meeting**, which is precisely the documented pre-hand-off state the split exists to support. Worse, the same change added a re-nag at `ready-for-tax3.ts:276` gated on `!tax_planner_id && tax_team_member_id`, which the line-72 guard made **unreachable** — dead code that read as a working feature. **Before splitting an overloaded column, grep every read of it and sort them into "meant the specific thing" and "meant anybody", because only the first group is safe to leave keyed on the narrowed column** — and treat an error MESSAGE naming both meanings (*"Team Member / Tax Planner"*) as the loudest possible tell that a guard is in the second group. A migration that moves rows out of the old column turns each such guard into an immediate live regression on exactly the rows it moved.

**How it was caught, and why that is the reusable part.** Nothing in the feature work found this — not the build, not `deno check`, not the live click-through, which exercised the *new* paths and never re-walked the old ones. **It surfaced during the wrap-up DOC AUDIT**, from reading `ready-for-tax3.ts`'s guard while rewriting the allocation section and noticing its error string promised something the code no longer allowed. That is the argument for auditing docs against the diff rather than from memory: the doc sentence *"a Team Member is allocated, and Tray books the ROI meeting off the back of that allocation"* was a written-down invariant, and checking it against the code is what exposed the break. **Fixed in the same session** — both guards widened to `!tax_planner_id && !tax_team_member_id`, and `skip-roi-meeting.ts`'s explicit `.select(...)` gained `tax_team_member_id`, without which the added term would have read `undefined` and left the guard permanently true (#448 nested inside the fix for #452). **Zero live occurrences:** the only exposed row, `client_tax_plans` 159, was DB-verified after the fix with `ready_for_tax3_email_sent` / `roi_meeting_skipped_at` / `tax3_meeting_date` all still NULL, so neither guard ever fired in the v793 window.

**453. A GENERATED ARTIFACT THAT MIRRORS A MONEY PROCESS MUST IMPORT THAT PROCESS'S CONSTANTS, NEVER RE-TYPE THEM — AND WHEN TWO INDEPENDENT GENERATION-TIME SURGERIES LAND ON ONE PART, THEIR ORDER IS A CONTRACT AND EACH NEEDS ITS OWN ASSERTION, OR THEIR FAILURES MASK EACH OTHER (2026-08-27, `vfo-admin-api` v795 / ROI template v6). ALWAYS-APPLIES (generated artifacts, money mirrors). Extends #336, #374, #342.**

Two lessons from one change — teaching the client-facing ROI deck the revised tax fee process. Both generalise well past the deck.

**(1) THE MIRROR MUST IMPORT, NOT RESTATE.** The deck prints a fee schedule the money engine also computes: 2 payments at or below $30,000, 3 above it, with a flat $15,000 initial retainer on the 3-payment shape. Those two numbers already existed as `THREE_PAYMENT_THRESHOLD` and `INITIAL_RETAINER_FLAT` in `constants/tax-fee-process.ts`. Re-typing them in `generate-presentation.ts` would have worked perfectly and been perfectly wrong: the day someone moves the threshold, the money moves and **the deck keeps quoting the old boundary to the client's face**, with no error, no failing gate and no test that could catch it — the deck is generated weeks before the payments run, so the disagreement surfaces as a client asking why the slide said something different. **The rule: when a client-facing artifact restates a rule the system enforces elsewhere, it must IMPORT the constant that encodes the rule.** Two copies of a number that must agree will eventually disagree, and the one nobody executes is the one that goes stale. The corollary matters too — **importing the constants is not the same as calling the function.** `deriveFeeSplit()` throws outside $0–$60,000, and a legacy plan with an out-of-band assess fee must still generate a deck, so the split is computed locally in whole cents with the sum identity asserted in code. **Import the FACTS, borrow the ARITHMETIC, and do not inherit the caller's guard rails when your failure mode is different from theirs.**

**(2) TWO SURGERIES ON ONE PART NEED A DEFINED ORDER AND INDEPENDENT EVIDENCE.** Slide 24 now gets up to two raw-XML operations before substitution: `dropFeeRow3()` (2-payment — delete the third fee row, un-shift the section beneath it) and `dropInvestYearsHeader()` (single-year — delete the year header, shift everything below it up). **The fee surgery MUST run first.** It selects the shapes to un-shift by an ABSOLUTE y threshold, and the header surgery would already have moved those shapes 539650 EMU above it — run the other way round, the threshold matches nothing and the count assert fires **loudly but for entirely the wrong reason**, sending the next reader to re-measure a constant that was never wrong.

The trap underneath that is the one worth internalising. Because the fee surgery changes how many shapes sit below the header, the header surgery's expected count became **mode-dependent** (`SHIFT_EXPECTED_2PAY` / `SHIFT_EXPECTED_3PAY` — **9 / 10 since template v8; they were 12 / 14 on v6 and v7, see #480**). The tempting implementation is to have the second surgery COUNT what it finds, or derive its expectation from what the first one did. **That is exactly the shape that makes an assertion worthless:** if the row surgery silently no-ops, a derived expectation quietly agrees with it and the deck ships with a hole in it. So the count is **PASSED IN** from the fee mode instead — the two surgeries assert on independent evidence, and either one failing alone is still a loud 500. **The rule: composing transformations over a shared mutable structure is only safe when (a) the order is written down at the call site and justified, and (b) every step's assertion is derivable WITHOUT reference to the steps around it.** An assertion that reads the state a previous step produced is testing that the code did what it did, not that it did what it should.

**A third, smaller note from the same change, in the "prove it, don't scan for it" family (#410):** the shape the fee surgery had to move on slide 24 carries its text in an `<a:t xml:space="preserve">` run, which a naive `<a:t>` search misses entirely. When locating shapes in OOXML by their text, match the element with its attributes allowed, or locate by shape id — and assert the count either way.

---

**454. COPYING AN ID TO ROUTE THE MONEY ALSO SILENTLY RE-POINTS EVERY OTHER SURFACE THAT READS THAT ID — A "MARKER ONLY" COLUMN BESIDE A LOAD-BEARING ONE STILL HAS TO GATE ALL OF THEM (2026-08-27, `vfo-admin-api` v796, `members.stripe_account_linked_from`). ALWAYS-APPLIES (money, auth). Cousin of #452, #318.**

A Corporate Member (`58147-C1`) has no Stripe Connect account, so their rev-share should pay into their LEAD member's account (`58147`). The cheapest correct-looking implementation is a **copy**: write the lead's `stripe_account_id` onto the corporate member's row. It genuinely works — all four payout engines (`actions/pipeline/contract-revshare.ts`, `actions/tax/revshare.ts`, `actions/msm/pip-revshare.ts`, `utils/specialist-revenue-payout.ts`) read `members.stripe_account_id` directly as the Stripe transfer `destination`, so **the copy IS the routing** and not one engine had to change.

**That is exactly what makes it dangerous.** `stripe_account_id` is not read only by the money. It is read by every surface that means *"this member's own Stripe account"*, and a copy turns each of those into a statement about **someone else's** account with no visible difference at the read site:

1. **`actions/members/stripe-connect-request.ts`** — the setup-email button would mail the corporate member an onboarding link **into the lead's Stripe account**, letting them submit or change the lead's banking details.
2. **`actions/payouts/connect-setup-link.ts`** — the same hole reached from the PUBLIC `/payout-setup?token=` page. Worse than the first, because that token is **durable and already sitting in an inbox**: closing only the admin button leaves the door open. It returns the generic 404 (reason logged, never leaked).
3. **`actions/payments/member-payments-load.ts`** — the payout history lists Stripe transfers **by destination**, so the corporate member's Payments tab would show the lead's entire payout history *and every sibling corporate member's*. A read-only leak, and the one nobody would have looked for.

The marker column exists solely so those three can tell the difference; nothing resolves through it. **The rule: when you make one row borrow another row's identifier, the marker you add is not documentation — it is a gate, and it owes a check to every surface that reads the borrowed column, not just the ones that spend money.** Enumerate the readers of the load-bearing column FIRST and sort them into "meant the value" (unaffected) and "meant *whose it is*" (all now wrong) — the second group is where the bugs are, and none of them will fail a type-check, a build, or a click-through of the feature you just built. Two of the three holes here are *write* surfaces into a third party's account and one is a *read* leak; a link feature that guards only the obvious write is still broken.

**The unlink is the same lesson from the other end:** clearing the link must clear the copy, and must REFUSE when there is no marker — an unlink on a member with a genuinely-owned account would throw the real account id away. And linking must refuse when `stripe_account_id` is set with no marker, for the same reason. The lead is resolved **server-side** from the member row (`connected_member_number`, else the member-number prefix); a lead id taken from the body would let a caller point any member's payouts at any account.

---

**455. BEING IN `MEMBER_SCOPED_ACTIONS` IS NOT AN OWNERSHIP GUARD — IT REWRITES `body.member_number`, SO A HANDLER THAT KEYS ON A DIFFERENT BODY ID IS COMPLETELY UNGUARDED WHILE LOOKING GATED (2026-08-27, `vfo-admin-api` v796, `msm_load_clients` IDOR). ALWAYS-APPLIES (auth/security). The mirror of #309; sharpens #142.**

`msm_load_clients` sat in `MEMBER_SCOPED_ACTIONS`, which reads as "scoped to the caller" at every glance — the list's name says so and every neighbouring action in it genuinely is. But that list does exactly ONE thing: the middleware **overwrites `body.member_number` with the caller's own**. `msm_load_clients` never reads `member_number`. It reads `body.enrollment_id`, joins `client_enrollments`, and returns whole `clients.*` rows — names, emails, phones. Enrollment ids are **sequential integers**. Any logged-in member could enumerate any other member's entire client list by incrementing a number, and the gate list would have kept saying the action was member-scoped the whole time. `msm_load_training_progress` had the identical shape.

Both now take `auth` and call the pre-existing `denyIfNotOwnEnrollment` (`utils/client-ownership.ts`) as their first statement, with `dispatch.ts` passing `c.auth`. `msm_load_training_track` was checked and needs nothing — it keys on `program_id` and returns shared curriculum templates.

**The rule: a gate list is only a guard for the body key it actually constrains. Before trusting membership of `MEMBER_SCOPED_ACTIONS`, read the handler and name the body field it filters on** — if that field is anything other than `member_number`, the action is as unguarded as one in no list at all, and needs an in-handler ownership check like any other. This is #309 read from the opposite side: there, "in no list" looked safe and was not; here, "in the scoped list" looked safe and was not. **The common failure is treating a list's NAME as its semantics.** The same question applies to every other rewrite-style gate and every handler keyed on `client_id`, `ciq_id`, `plan_id` or `enrollment_id` — the correct audit is "what does this handler filter on", never "which list is it in".

A note on how it was found and what that costs: this surfaced during **unrelated exploration** of the member portal, not from any gate, review or report — `deno check`, the build and every live click-through pass on it, because a straight IDOR breaks nothing for the legitimate caller. And it shipped **code-only: no forged-enrollment call was ever attempted**, so the fix is reasoned, not exercised.

---

**456. A RECURRING SCHEDULE MUST RE-ANCHOR FROM THE DAY THE CHARGE ACTUALLY HAPPENS, NEVER FROM THE STORED DUE DATE — AN OVERDUE-THEN-FUNDED ROW OTHERWISE GETS CHARGED ONCE PER SWEEP TICK UNTIL IT "CATCHES UP" (2026-08-27, `vfo-admin-api` v797, `automation_GC_recurring_sweep`). Narrow — timed re-anchoring flows only; registry, not the hub's curated list.**

The recurring Growth Credit sweep advances `gc_subscriptions.next_charge_date` after every successful charge. As first written it advanced it **from the row's own `next_charge_date`** — `addInterval(sub.next_charge_date, interval)` — which is the obvious reading of "one period after the last one" and is correct for the only case anybody tests: a row that is due *today*.

It is wrong for every row that is due in the **past**, and this feature is built to produce exactly those. A subscription that runs out of credits goes `on_hold` with its due date deliberately frozen, so a member who tops up six weeks later has a row six periods overdue. Re-anchoring from the stored date moves it to *five* periods overdue — still `<= today` — so the next night's sweep charges again, and the night after that, and so on until the arithmetic catches up with the calendar. Six nightly charges for one resumed subscription, each one individually looking like a correct single period, with the balance draining and the member getting a renewal email every morning.

**The tell that this is worth its own gotcha: the code comment above the line already said "re-anchor from the charge day".** The comment was written first, described the right behaviour, and the expression underneath it quietly used the other variable. Nothing catches that — not `deno check`, not the build, not a click-through, and not a same-day sweep test, because when `next_charge_date == today` the two expressions are **identical**. Caught in review before deploy; the fix is `addInterval(todayIso, …)`, and the behaviour was then live-proven with a row hand-set due `2026-07-15`, funded on the 27th: charged once, re-anchored to `2026-09-27`.

**The rules.** (a) In any date-advancing scheduler, the anchor is the **event**, not the schedule — write the period forward from when the thing actually happened, so missed periods can never stack. (b) A hold-and-resume path needs a test where the due date is **weeks past, not equal to today**; the equal-to-today case cannot distinguish the two implementations and will pass either way. (c) Treat a comment that agrees with your intent as evidence of nothing — read the expression and name which variable it reads. The same shape lives anywhere a `next_*_date`, a retry backoff or a renewal anniversary is recomputed from a stored value that may already be stale.

---

**457. A MONEY INPUT PARSED WITH BARE `parseFloat` BESIDE A SIBLING THAT STRIPS `[$,\s]` IS A LIVE BUG WEARING A PLAUSIBLE FALLBACK — `NaN || 0` RENDERS "TBD" AND STORES NULL WITH NO ERROR ANYWHERE (2026-08-27, `vfo-admin-api` v798, `automation_TAX_decision`). Narrow — free-text money fields; registry, not the hub's curated list.**

The Tax 3 Undecided decision form takes two money figures side by side. `initialRetainer` went through `parseFeeTotal`, which strips `[$,\s]` before parsing. `potentialTaxSavings` went through a bare `parseFloat(fd.potentialTaxSavings)`. Neither field constrains what an admin may type, and an admin typed **`$757,805`**.

`parseFloat("$757,805")` is `NaN`. The handler used it twice, and both uses had a fallback that made the failure look like an ordinary empty field: `parseFloat(...) || null` at the `client_tax_plans.potential_tax_savings` write, so the column stored **NULL**, and `parseFloat(...) || 0` at the `[TAX_SAVINGS]` token, where a zero renders the deliberate **`"TBD"`** placeholder. The client's decision email therefore went out reading *"$TBD"* of projected savings — **a legitimate, designed output for a figure nobody had entered**, on a plan where the figure had been entered correctly. Nothing threw, nothing logged, and the retainer figure beside it was perfect, which is exactly what makes it read as data entry rather than code. It was reported by a human looking at the email, and confirmed only by reading `client_tax_progress` notes to find what had actually been typed.

**The rules.** (a) **Sanitize at the PARSE, not at the form.** A frontend strip is worth adding — this change added one — but it is a convenience: the handler is the boundary, it can be reached by a stale bundle or a chained call, and until it is deployed *and* the frontend is deployed the backend is the only guard that exists. (b) **When one money field in a handler gains a sanitizer, audit every sibling in the same payload in the same change.** The asymmetry here was not a regression; the two fields were simply written at different times by different people and never compared. Grep the handler for `parseFloat(` and require every hit to name a sanitizing helper. (c) **A `|| 0` / `|| null` fallback behind a numeric parse converts a parse failure into a valid-looking business state**, and the more thoughtfully that state was designed — a "TBD" placeholder, a nullable column — the more completely it hides the bug. Where a fallback is genuinely wanted, branch on `Number.isNaN` first so "unparseable" and "not supplied" stay distinguishable, the same three-state discipline as **#451**. (d) The data damage outlives the fix: the column was repaired by SQL, but **Gmail drafts are minted once and never regenerated**, so the already-sent email had to be corrected by hand. Cross-ref **#438** (the other way a money substitution silently corrupts itself), **#448**, **#451**.

---

**458. A BOLDSIGN `document/send` THAT RETURNS 200 + A documentId IS NOT PROOF THE DOCUMENT EXISTS — IT CAN VANISH ASYNCHRONOUSLY, AND EVERY DOWNSTREAM 403 THEN LOOKS EXACTLY LIKE THE NORMAL CREATE RACE**

Lana Hurdle's Tax 3 agreement send (client 159, plan 107, 2026-08-28 14:52:35Z) got a clean `200` from BoldSign `document/send` with `documentId f8cf9e52-73ac-4fac-bbe1-51cb5e3a0209`. The document never materialised: it is absent from the BoldSign dashboard, and all five `getEmbeddedSignLink` attempts returned 403. The email shipped to the client with the red `[ENGAGEMENT — signing link unavailable]` placeholder where the signing button belongs, and `agreement_sent='Yes'` / `agreement_sent_at` / `boldsign_doc_id` were all written anyway — so the signing-reminder ladder armed itself against a document that does not exist and began chasing a client who had no link to click.

Ruled out, each checked: the discount field (NULL, and `send-agreement.ts` never reads it), the blank `risk_mindset` (substitutes to `""`, cannot fail the call), sandbox/env confusion (TAX sandbox off, live key), and the template `field_map` (all four rows page-4; row 8 of the same template produced a working document for Chris Colby from the same live key the day before). What is left is a transient BoldSign-side async failure AFTER the API accepted the request.

The trap is that the 403 is **ambiguous by design**. A newly created BoldSign document legitimately 403s for a few seconds, which is exactly why the retry loop exists — so a permanent 403 caused by a missing document is indistinguishable from the benign race the loop was written for. The loop exhausts its budget, shrugs, and the handler proceeds down its success path.

**The rules.** (a) **A 403 loop that EXHAUSTS its budget is evidence about the DOCUMENT, not the timing** — treat exhaustion as "the document is probably not there", not "it was slow". (b) **Never write a success flag over an email you know shipped broken.** `agreement_sent='Yes'` beside an empty signing link is a false record, and it is the flag the reminder ladders read. (c) The identical loop and the identical silent failure live in MAP 1's `contract-send-agreement.ts` — a fix to one is owed to the other. (d) Remediation for a stuck row is to CLEAR the decision step (delete the `client_tax_progress` row, NULL `tax_decision` / `agreement_sent` / `agreement_sent_at` / `boldsign_doc_id` / `client_signed` / `ceo_signed` / `signed_followup_sent_date`, keep the fee columns) so the step can be re-answered and the ladder disarms.

**FLAGGED, NOT BUILT (2026-08-28):** the 5×5s retry budget is unchanged; nothing records or alerts when the link comes back empty; both pipelines still write the success flag regardless.

**459. A mode-keyed CATCH-ALL row placed mid-renderer silently preempts every branch below it, and the branches below keep their own now-dead handling for that mode (2026-08-31).** `TaxPrioritiesTab.jsx`'s `renderTaskInner` carries `if (readOnly) return <generic row>` roughly 340 lines above the allocation branch, so in the MEMBER view every step declared after that line renders through the generic row instead of its own. The generic row colours its dot from `statusColors[p.status]`. **The failure was invisible for every step but one, and that is the trap:** those steps store a status-VOCABULARY word (`Completed`, `Proceed`, `Yes`), which the map knows, so they looked right. The allocation step stores a **person's NAME** (`allocate-planner.ts` writes `rosterFullName(...)` into the progress row), `statusColors['Katie Williams']` is `undefined`, and the dot fell to the muted-grey fallback on a completed step. **Two rules.** (a) A catch-all keyed on a MODE must sit AFTER every branch that renders that mode itself — otherwise those branches' `readOnly` arms are dead code that reads as live (`tax_deposit_pi`, `tax_3_decision`, post-review and additional-info all still carry such arms, unreachable, deliberately left). Fixing this by MOVING the catch-all would silently switch four other steps to a different member rendering; the safe fix is to exempt the one step by a named predicate (`isAllocTask`). (b) **Never feed a free-text column to a status→colour/label map.** A column that can hold a name has no vocabulary, so every lookup is a coin flip that fails to the fallback — and a fallback colour is exactly what "not done" looks like. Related: #448 (a predicate is only as good as its input), #392 (a displayed label is not the stored value).

**460. The tax ALLOCATION step has THREE done-tests and they disagree on a team-member-only plan — and the docs asserted they agreed (2026-08-31).** After the 2026-08-26 two-slot split, "is the allocation done?" is answered in three places: (a) the step's own dot in `TaxPrioritiesTab.jsx` — `isAllocated = !!(tax_planner_id || tax_team_member_id)`, **either slot**; (b) `isTaskStatused` in the SAME component — `livePlan?.tax_planner_id != null`, **planner slot only**, and it is what feeds the phase pills, the `TrackHero` x-of-y hero count and `alreadyDone`; (c) the backend `utils/tax-plan-steps.ts`, where `tax_planner_select` has **no dedicated branch at all** and falls to the catch-all reading the progress row — which `allocate-planner.ts` writes on either slot, so **done**. On a plan holding only a Team Member (live examples: plans 159 and 162, Katie Williams) the step shows a green dot, the phase pill counts it unfinished, and Client Overview calls it finished. `flows/tax-planning.md` claimed *"either slot closes the step … backend `buildTaxPlanSteps` and the FE agree"* — false for (b), and corrected. **#339 makes the two REPOS mirror each other; this is the same failure INSIDE one component**, which no cross-repo check would ever catch. When a column is split, grep for every done-test of the affected step and enumerate them — a component may own more than one, and the one driving the visible dot is rarely the one driving the counts.

**461. AN ERROR SURFACE GATED ON A STATE THE FAILING ACTION ITSELF WOULD HAVE CHANGED SWALLOWS EVERY REFUSAL ON AN ALREADY-ANSWERED STEP — THE USER SEES "NOTHING HAPPENED" WHILE THE SERVER CORRECTLY REFUSED (2026-09-01, `TaxPrioritiesTab.jsx` `AmendFeeStep`). Narrow — one render gate; registry, not the hub's curated list.**

The amend-fee card rendered its server-refusal message as `{!answered && submitError && <div>…</div>}`. The `!answered` term looks like ordinary tidiness — do not clutter a completed step with an error — and it was written when the only way to reach the card was an unanswered step.

The card is **re-openable**. "Change amount" lets an admin re-amend a step they have already answered, and that is precisely the request the server is most likely to refuse: the money moved on, the charge went `processing`, or (as of the same session) VFO `cancelled` it. Every one of those refusals is a correct 400 with a written explanation — and `answered` is true on exactly those rows, so the message was mounted behind a condition that the failing path guarantees to be false. Clicking Save did nothing visible: no spinner change the eye catches, no error, no write. Found only because the session was deliberately provoking the new `cancelled` guard and knew a refusal was owed.

**The rules.** (a) **Render a refusal unconditionally.** The gate belongs on the ACTION — decide whether the button may be pressed — never on the message that explains why pressing it failed. If a message must be scoped, scope it to the request that produced it, not to a row state. (b) **Any condition on an error surface that names a state the action mutates is suspect by construction**: `!answered`, `!saved`, `!complete`, `isDraft`. Ask which value that flag holds on the failure path, and if the answer is "the one that hides the message", the gate is inverted. (c) A silent no-op is worse than the refusal it hides — the admin's next move is to try again, or to conclude the feature is broken, and neither reaches the sentence the handler already wrote. Related: **#403** (a UI lock is choreography; the handler 400 is the boundary — but the 400 has to be *visible* to be worth writing), **#187**.

**462. A PREREQUISITE GATE PUT IN FRONT OF A DELIBERATELY RE-RUNNABLE HANDLER MUST BE SCOPED TO THE WINDOW IT PROTECTS, OR EVERY RE-RUN ON A ROW THAT PREDATES THE GATE 400s FOREVER ON A STEP THAT CAN NO LONGER BE ANSWERED (2026-09-01, `vfo-admin-api` v802, `actions/tax/implement-decision.ts`).**

Enabling the amend steps for legacy tax plans meant de-gating the two "answer the amend step first" backstops from `isNewFeeProcess`, so they applied on every fee shape. On Tax 4 that is the whole change: `postreview-decision.ts` returns its idempotency response before the gate is reached, so a plan whose decision is already recorded never evaluates it.

**Tax 5 is not that handler.** `automation_TAX_implementdecision` is re-run by design — an admin who recorded *Undecided* comes back and flips it to *Proceed* through the same call — so the gate IS reached on rows whose `implementation_decision` is already set. And on exactly those rows the amend step is **unanswerable**: `amend-fee.ts` refuses stage `tax5` once the decision exists. An un-scoped gate would therefore have wedged every legacy plan that was past Tax 5 at the moment of enablement, permanently, on an instruction the admin could not follow — *"Complete the 'Amend implementation fee' step first"* against a step with no answerable action behind it. The fix is one term: `if (!plan.implementation_decision && plan.fee_amended_at_tax5 == null)`. Once the decision is recorded, amendment is closed and the gate has nothing left to protect.

**The rules.** (a) **Before adding a prerequisite, ask whether the handler behind it can run twice.** A gate is safe in front of a one-shot write; in front of a re-entrant one it is a permanent condition on a permanent state. (b) **Scope the gate to the window in which the prerequisite is still satisfiable** — the same columns the prerequisite's own handler guards on, so "required" can never outlive "answerable". This is the gate-side twin of the applicability rule the step machine got in the same change. (c) **The wedge is invisible at ship.** No build, no `deno check`, no click-through of the new feature touches it: the tested path is a fresh plan inside the window, and the broken path is an old row nobody flips until weeks later. Enumerate the rows that already exist in the state the gate now inspects — the count (~9 plans in the Tax-5 window, ~22 past it) is the test case. Related: **#403**, **#414** (a migrated row never got the column your branch keys on), **#452**.

**463. A MONEY THRESHOLD THAT IS ONE CONSTANT WITH A STRICT COMPARISON DEFINES EXACTLY TWO BANDS. ADDING A THIRD NEEDS TWO NAMED EDGES AND ONE SHARED, NON-THROWING CORE THAT EVERY CONSUMER IMPORTS — AND ANY DISPLAY LABEL MUST BE DERIVED FROM THE ARITHMETIC, NEVER FROM THE SHAPE FLAG (2026-09-01, `vfo-admin-api` v805, `constants/tax-fee-process.ts`).**

The revised tax fee process shipped on 2026-08-25 with `THREE_PAYMENT_THRESHOLD = 30000` and one test, `cents > THREE_PAYMENT_THRESHOLD * 100`. One constant, one comparison, two bands — and six consumers quietly took those two bands to be the whole vocabulary. Then a real engagement was priced at **$30,119** and split `$15,000` initial / **`$59.50`** final / `$15,059.50` implementation. The arithmetic was correct; the second payment was absurd, and a client was sent a 3-payment agreement asking for fifty-nine dollars and fifty cents.

**The rules.**

(a) **Two edges, both named.** A band has a floor AND a ceiling, so a third band needs a SECOND constant — `THREE_PAYMENT_MIN = 31000` beside the existing `THREE_PAYMENT_THRESHOLD = 30000` — and both boundaries stay strict, so *"what happens at exactly $30,000"* and *"what happens at exactly $31,000"* are both answerable from the constants alone rather than from someone's choice of `>` or `>=`. The band's WIDTH is a product decision with an arithmetic consequence worth stating in the comment: here it is what makes `$500` the smallest final retainer that can now exist.

(b) **ONE core, non-throwing, imported by every consumer — and enumerate the consumers BEFORE deciding where it lives.** They were pricing (`deriveFeeSplit`), the amend handler, the ROI-deck generator, the invoice label builder, the agreement body selector and the frontend mirror. One of them — the deck — must still render for a legacy assess fee outside the `$0–$60,000` cap, which is the proof that the core has to be **validation-free** and that the range check belongs in the dollars-facing wrapper, not in the shared function. Any consumer that re-derives the band from the raw constants instead of calling the core is a second copy of the rule, and the copy nobody executes is the one that goes stale (**#453**).

(c) **A DISPLAY LABEL must come from the ARITHMETIC, not from the shape flag.** The tax invoice printed *"Retainer (50%)"* on every plan that was not 3-payment, because for as long as the code had existed *"not 3-payment"* had meant *"an even split"*. A buffer-band plan is 2-payment, unamended and undiscounted — and `$15,000` of a `$30,999` total is nowhere near half, so **no flag in the system described it** and the document would have contradicted itself in writing, to the client. The truthful test is the sum itself — `retainer x 2 === total` in whole cents, with a 1c tolerance for the rounding a genuine odd-total even split produces — and it catches every future shape no flag describes.

(d) **Reuse an existing STORAGE shape if one exists, rather than inventing a column or a flag.** A band plan is written exactly like a plan a Tax 4 amendment converted back to 2 payments (retainer = initial = the flat amount, final NULL), so the existing `final_retainer_amount` predicate classifies it correctly with **no change at any consumer**, and the existing conversion arm turns it into a 3-payment plan if a later amendment reaches the floor. A new column or a new boolean would have been a new branch in every one of those six places, each an opportunity for **#448**. Related: **#453**, **#440**, **#448**.

**464. AN FE MIRROR OF A SERVER-SIDE MONEY DERIVATION CANNOT BE CLICK-TESTED AGAINST AN UNDEPLOYED BACKEND — THE DEV SERVER HITS THE LIVE FUNCTION, SO THE FORM PREVIEWS THE NEW RULE WHILE THE SERVER STILL WRITES THE OLD ONE, AND THE PASS LOOKS GREEN (2026-09-01).**

The portal's dev server runs deliberately with **no `VITE_API_URL` override** — it talks to the LIVE `vfo-admin-api`. Every money form in the portal previews its figures from a frontend COPY of a server derivation: `TaxPrioritiesTab.jsx`'s `splitFeeCents` / `deriveFeeSplit` / `amendFeePreview` mirror `constants/tax-fee-process.ts`, and `MemberGCMarketplace.jsx` mirrors `GC_PACKAGES`. So on a branch that changes both halves, a click-through taken BEFORE the backend deploy renders the NEW preview and then submits it to a server still running the OLD rule.

**What makes it dangerous is that it looks like a pass.** The preview shows `$15,000` / `$15,900`, the save returns `200`, no error appears anywhere — and the row that lands is `15,000` / `59.50` / `15,059.50`. Nothing in the build, the type-check or the screen distinguishes the two.

**The rules.** (a) **On any branch changing a derivation on BOTH sides of the repo boundary, deploy the BACKEND FIRST and click through afterwards.** This branch deployed `v805` before its live pass for exactly this reason. It is the same ordering rule as **#417** (BE-before-FE), reached from the other end: there the frontend would have called an action that did not exist yet; here it calls one whose ANSWER has changed. (b) **A green preview is evidence about the FE copy and nothing else** — until the backend is deployed, the click-through has verified one of the two mirrors and asserted nothing at all about the one that owns the money. (c) **Read the ROW, not the screen.** The verification of a derivation change is a `SELECT` of what was actually written; the preview and the write come from different code on different machines, and a branch like this is precisely when they disagree. Related: **#339**, **#417**, **#145**.
**465. A ROW RENDERED AS "THE RESIDUAL" SILENTLY ABSORBS EVERY PARTY THE RENDERER HAS NEVER HEARD OF, AND CLAMPING THAT RESIDUAL AT ZERO DESTROYS THE ONE SIGNAL THAT WOULD HAVE EXPOSED IT (2026-09-02).**

A tax plan pays up to four parties. `tax/PricingSplitCard.jsx` listed three and computed the fourth cell — VFO Services — as *"this payment, less member, less planner"*. Introduce a strategic partner and its share lands in that cell: `$1,466.25` shown as VFO income where `$488.75` of it was Tax Plan IQ's and had already been transferred to them. **The column still summed to its header**, because a residual always does. That is the whole trap — a residual is defined to make the arithmetic close, so it launders every unknown leg into itself and reports a total that reconciles perfectly against a breakdown that is wrong.

**(a) Enumerate the PAYEES from the payout engine, never from the columns the component already renders.** The engine (`actions/tax/revshare.ts` + `utils/tax-planner-payout.ts` + `utils/strategic-partner-payout.ts`) knows four legs; the card knew two-plus-a-remainder. The sibling `map1/Map1PricingSplitCard.jsx` had carried a conditional strategic row from the day it was written, so the correct shape existed in the codebase, one directory away, for the entire life of the bug. **A residual cell is a claim that you have enumerated everything else** — treat adding one as the moment to go and check.

**(b) A residual clamped with `Math.max(x, 0)` converts an over-booked row into a plausible `$0.00`.** The Accounting panel booked Lorente's member leg at `$3,750` and the partner at `$750` against `$2,443.75` and `$488.75` actually paid, which drove its VFO residual to **−$590**. The clamp rendered that as a clean zero in a column of other clean numbers. The negative WAS the alarm and the clamp ate it. **Clamp a residual only where a negative is genuinely impossible; where it is possible, a negative must surface — it is a free, exact assertion that the parts agree with the whole.** The `$0.00` was the only visible symptom of a row that over-booked its own RECEIVED by `$590`, and it read as "VFO earned nothing on this one".

**(c) When several surfaces prorate the same money, ONE of them being right is not evidence about the others — and it is usually the newest that is right.** `taxShared.jsx` held three helpers: `plannerPortion` used `(share / total) * payment` and matched the engine; `memberPortion` and `stratPer` hard-split `share / 2`. A `/ 2` split is not a statement about the share, it is a statement about the **payment shape** — that the retainer and implementation are equal halves — and it was correct for every plan in the system until the 2026-09-01 buffer band made uneven splits ordinary (**#463**). The planner helper was right only because it was written last, after the convention was documented. **Collapse every such helper onto one shared function that quotes the engine, fallback included**, and take the divergence between two co-located helpers as a dating clue, not a design.

**(d) Delete a dead branch rather than porting it, but prove it is dead with a census.** `memberPortion` also carried `share <= 100 ? (share / 100) * amount`, a percentage interpretation. `revshare.ts` says in terms that there is none, and a count over the 58 non-stopped plans found **0** shares small enough to reach it. Both halves matter: the engine comment is the intent and the census is the fact, and a "legacy support" branch nothing can reach is a live invitation to preserve a convention the money never had.

**(e) Measure the blast radius with a query before deciding a display bug is minor OR major.** Exactly two live plans have an uneven retainer/implementation split, one booked and one not yet — which is what made a fix to a money-reporting surface safe to ship after eyeballing a single month. Related: **#252**, **#394**, **#463**, **#453**, **#448**.

**466. A SECOND WRITER THAT REUSES AN EXISTING TYPE VALUE CONFLATES THE TWO EVENTS PERMANENTLY, AND EVERY READER INHERITS THE LIE — INCLUDING THE ONES THAT TALK TO CUSTOMERS (2026-09-03).**

`gc_add_credits` — the admin *"add credits"* button — writes `gc_transactions.type = "purchased"` for a **comp**, the same value `fulfillGrowthCredits` writes for a real Stripe sale. Nothing downstream can undo that: the column says sold, no money was taken, and the two are byte-identical on the field every reader keys on. It ran for months and surfaced only when a member reported buying credits that appear nowhere in Stripe — **he was reading the portal correctly**. 31 of 33 `purchased` rows were grants, across **27 members**, and the *"Credits Sold"* tile read **3,257** when **2** credits had ever been sold.

**(a) When you add a second writer to a typed column, give it its OWN value.** The cost of not doing so is not a wrong label, it is that the distinction no longer exists in the data. Retrofitting it means finding an *incidental* discriminator and teaching every reader — here `stripe_session_id`, present on a sale and NULL on a grant, which worked only because Stripe's own artifact happened to be stored. Had it not been, the only fix would have been a migration plus a guess about history.

**(b) The reader that was already right tells you what the test should be.** `revenue_usd` in `load-accounting.ts` was correct throughout, because it summed `amount_usd` — a field only a payment populates — instead of branching on the type. The money figure was right while the credit count beside it was wrong, in the same loop, for the same rows. **When two totals derived from one collection disagree in trustworthiness, the honest one is keyed on a field the event itself produces; copy its test.** Same shape as **#465**, one layer earlier: #465 is the reader inventing a label, this is the writer destroying the distinction.

**(c) Fix at the readers unless you are prepared to migrate.** The row is still written `purchased`; the label, the tile split and the member history all derive from `stripe_session_id` now. That is retroactive and needs no backfill — but it means **anything new reading `gc_transactions` must apply the same test**, so the rule lives in `tables/marketplace-gc.md` beside the column, not only in the handlers.

**(d) Two lists in two repos that must agree are the same failure with a longer fuse.** `SALES_TEAM_NAMES` (react `NewModelSaleModal.jsx`) decides who APPEARS in the *"Team Member Responsible"* dropdown; `TEAM_MEMBER_LOGIN_EMAILS` (edge `constants/onboarding-team.ts`) decides where that person's bells LAND. A name added to only the first is selectable and then routes to the shared `admin` bell — no error, no missing-field symptom, and the person simply never hears about their own onboarding. Move both in one change, and check the name has an `allowed_admins` row or the bell is addressed to a login that cannot see it (#60).

**(e) An action with no `created_by` and no log answers no questions later.** `gc_transactions` records what happened and never who did it; `add-credits.ts` logs nothing, and there is no dispatch- or auth-level request logging, so *"who granted these credits?"* is unanswerable — five admins held live sessions at the timestamp. **For any admin action that moves money, credits or entitlements, write the actor at the same moment you write the effect.** Related: **#465**, **#431**, **#402**, **#60**.

---

**467. AN ALLOWLIST ENTRY IS INERT WHILE AN ADMIN-ONLY STEP GATES THE STEP IT GRANTS — WIDEN THE GATE OR THE GRANT DOES NOTHING; AND WIDENING A ROLE ONTO A HANDLER THAT NEVER HAD ONE MEANS IT HAS NO OWNERSHIP GUARD (2026-09-03, v807). ALWAYS-APPLIES (roles / tax track).**

`Client decision 1` and `Implementation decision` have been in `TAX_PLANNER_ALLOWED_ACTIONS` and on both `PLANNER_EDITABLE_TASK_NAMES` whitelists for months. A planner still could not reach either of them, because the step DIRECTLY ABOVE each one — `Amend fee` (Tax 4) and `Amend implementation fee` (Tax 5b) — was admin-only, and each amend step GATES the decision below it on BOTH surfaces: `stepGate` draws the lock in `TaxPrioritiesTab.jsx`, and `postreview-decision.ts` / `implement-decision.ts` each return 400 while the amend step exists and is unanswered. A planner therefore dead-ended at Tax 4 with a grant that read as complete in every gate list. **Reading the allowlist tells you what a role may CALL; it does not tell you what a role can REACH.** When a grant appears not to work, walk the step ORDER and check what the surface immediately before it requires — the missing permission is often on a neighbour, not on the action you are looking at. This is the ordering-sensitive cousin of #403 (a lock is choreography) and #256 (a gate entry matching nothing protects nothing): here the entries matched, the guards were right, and the feature was still unusable.

**The security half, and the reason a widening is never a one-line list edit.** `amend-fee.ts` carried NO caller-ownership check, correctly — it had run admin-only since it was written, and the ADMIN_ONLY gate was its whole boundary. Adding the action to `TAX_PLANNER_ALLOWED_ACTIONS` alone would have made it a money IDOR the same instant: a planner session could re-price ANY plan in the system off a body `tax_plan_id`, moving the final retainer, the implementation charge and every revenue-share leg on a client in another group (verified pre-fix by reading the handler; verified post-fix by three 403s with the target plan's columns provably untouched). `denyIfNotPlannerPlan` went in as the first statement after argument validation — **ahead of the `keep` short-circuit as well**, so an out-of-scope caller cannot distinguish a plan that exists from one that does not. **The rule: when you widen a deny-by-default role onto a handler, grep that handler for an ownership guard FIRST. A handler that has only ever served one role has no reason to own one, and the allowlist you are editing is not an ownership check (#257 / #142 / #455).**

**Third: the step's WRITE PATH may be two calls, and both need widening.** The amend card fires `automation_TAX_amend_fee` and THEN a second `tax_save_task` to write the progress row, so the two amend task names had to join `PLANNER_EDITABLE_TASK_NAMES` in the same change (#262). Allowlisting only the action would have let a planner amend the fee and then 403 on the step row — and the failure is ASYMMETRIC, which is what makes it easy to miss in testing: a real amendment self-heals, because `fee_amended_at_tax4/_tax5` closes the step on its own, while **"Keep the fee as it is" writes nothing at all** and would have left the step permanently unanswerable and the decision below it permanently locked. Exercise the answer that writes NOTHING, not just the one that writes money.

**Deliberate and worth stating, because it is a policy choice and not an oversight:** both portal roles behind a `tax_planner` session get this — Tax Planner and Team Member alike — and an amendment re-scales `tax_planner_share`, the planner's OWN payout leg. That conflict of interest is accepted, not prevented; it is made auditable by `client_tax_fee_amendments.created_by`, which stamps the caller's session email and needed no change to work for a planner. Related: **#257**, **#262**, **#309**, **#403**, **#455**, **#462**.

**468. A SYSTEM THAT REPORTS WHAT IT INTENDED INSTEAD OF WHAT IT DID: THE THREE SHAPES (2026-09-04).**

One branch turned up the same failure three times in three unrelated areas, which is why it is worth one entry rather than three. In each case a surface asserted something the system had not actually done, and in each case the assertion was built from the wrong input.

**(a) A STATUS LINE COMPOSED BEFORE THE ACTION IT DESCRIBES IS A PREDICTION, NOT A REPORT.** `contract-chargescheduled-sweep.ts` wrote the bell *"Client emailed a payment link."* from nothing but the presence of a `checkout_token`, several statements ABOVE the draft call it was describing — and the template it drafts is `send_mode=false`, so no client had ever been emailed by that branch in its life. Lenny Genna's P3 sat `declined` for two weeks behind that sentence while the only email that could collect the money waited unsent in Gmail Drafts. **Compose an outcome string AFTER the operation, from its return value.** If the operation has no return value, that is the bug: `draftMap1InstallmentFailureEmail` returned `void`, so there was nothing truthful to say even if the author had wanted to. It now returns `"sent" | "drafted" | false` and rides `deliverRaw` rather than `gmailDraftFetch`, because `deliverRaw` surfaces `sent` from `maybeSendDraft` — so a `drafts.send` that FAILS reads as `"drafted"`, which is true (the draft is still there). **A boolean is not enough where a draft-mode system exists: "created" and "delivered" are different facts and #325 means they diverge by default on 219 of 230 templates.** The old code additionally returned success when the Gmail POST itself failed.

**(b) AN OMITTED PAYEE UNDER-SUMS AND TELLS YOU; A RESIDUAL THAT SWALLOWS ONE RECONCILES AND LIES.** This is **#465** on a second surface with the opposite symptom, and the contrast is the lesson. On `tax/PricingSplitCard.jsx` the VFO cell was computed as the remainder, so a strategic partner's `$488.75` landed in it and the column still summed perfectly to its header — a wrong breakdown with a right total. On the Payments table the same payee was simply MISSING from the emitted `revShare` object while the server had already netted it off, so VFO was correct and the parts summed to `$1,215` of a `$1,350` installment. **When you add a leg to a payout engine, grep for every surface that RENDERS the split, not just every surface that COMPUTES it** — the computation was right in both places here; only the enumeration of who gets named was wrong. And when auditing such a surface, **test that the legs sum to the row total**: it catches the omission shape for free, and it is exactly the assertion `Math.max(residual, 0)` destroys in the absorption shape.

**(c) A PRONOUN IS NOT A TOKEN, AND "I" IS THE WORST ONE TO SWAP.** Converting 82 first-person-singular occurrences across 41 `email_templates` rows to plural shipped one defect on the first pass: *"Nevertheless, I hope that…"* became *"Nevertheless, **We** hope…"* eleven times. **"I" is capitalised in every position; every replacement for it inherits the case rules of an ordinary word,** so a global swap silently capitalises mid-sentence wherever the original sat after a comma or a semicolon. The check that finds this is cheap and total: **group every occurrence by its preceding ~24 characters and read the groups**, rather than spot-checking rows — that reduced ~70 sites to a dozen distinct contexts and made the single bad one obvious. Two further rules from the same pass: **carry the following word(s) in the pattern** so verb agreement moves with the pronoun (`I am writing` → `We are writing`, never `We am writing`); and **the sender is not the only voice in a template** — three matches were BUTTON LABELS in the recipient's voice (*"I Have Further Questions"* ×2, *"Reset my passcode"*), where pluralising makes the reader speak for a group they are not part of. A find-and-replace over a copy column has to answer "who is talking?" per occurrence.

**The unifying rule: derive the report from the result.** (a) took its input from a precondition, (b) from the columns already on screen, (c) from a pattern instead of the rendered sentence. Related: **#465**, **#325**, **#324**, **#376**, **#431**, **#402**.

---

**469. A COLUMN DEFAULT IS A VALUE A READER CANNOT DISTINGUISH FROM A WRITTEN ONE — GATE EVERY DERIVED FIGURE ON THE EVENT THAT WRITES THE REAL VALUE (2026-09-08).**

`advisor_onboarding.payment_amount` carries a column `DEFAULT 4000`. It means "the total engagement value", and it is written for real at CEO countersign, when the engagement is priced. Every row that has not reached countersign therefore already holds a plausible, wrong `4000` — and nothing in the row says so.

The new Stage 2 info line derives a balance from it (`payment_amount - deposit_amount`), so an onboarding with a `$4,000` deposit and no countersign rendered **"nothing further due"** on a record whose price nobody had set yet. The arithmetic was right; the input was a default wearing a value's clothes.

**A default is indistinguishable from a write by every ordinary test** — not NULL, not zero, the right type, inside the plausible range, and it survives `select`, `??` and truthiness checks identically. Only the *event* separates them. The fix is not a sentinel value and not a NOT NULL: it is to **gate the derived display on the column that proves the write happened** (here `agreement_signed_by_ceo_at`), and to say so on screen — *"balance calculated at countersign"* — rather than to print a number the row cannot justify.

Generalises past this row: any `DEFAULT` on a column a human is supposed to fill later is a booby trap for the first consumer that reads it before they do. When you add such a default, name its "written for real" event in the same breath; when you consume such a column, ask what proves the value is a decision rather than a placeholder. Related: **#429** (a NULL that is itself the switch), **#414**, **#448**.

---

**470. A PROVIDER REDELIVERS ITS TERMINAL EVENT, SO AN UNCONDITIONAL `col = now()` IN THAT BRANCH RE-DATES HISTORY ON EVERY REDELIVERY — ONCE-ONLY WRITES NEED `existing || now` (2026-09-08).**

BoldSign redelivers `Completed`. The advisor and accountant branches in `router/webhooks.ts` wrote `agreement_signed_by_ceo_at: nowIso` unconditionally, so each redelivery silently moved the countersign date forward to whenever the redelivery happened. Both are now `row.agreement_signed_by_ceo_at || nowIso`.

**The failure is invisible by construction.** There is no error, no duplicate row, no count that changes — just a date that is quietly younger than the event it records, on the exact column downstream logic keys on (here: whether the balance figure may be shown at all, #469). It presents as "the stamp looks recent" and is easy to mistake for correct.

Three rules fall out. **(1) Redelivery is normal, not exceptional** — we already know this for money (#327, where the answer is a latch column); a *timestamp* needs the same defence and rarely gets one, because nothing about it looks like a side effect. **(2) `existing || now` is the whole fix and it costs one `select` column** — the row is usually already loaded. **(3) Read the branch for OTHER unconditional writes at the same time**: a stamp is the cheapest thing to get wrong, but any `= now()`, sequence allocation or append in a redeliverable branch has the same shape. Related: **#327**, **#384** (which is why these columns matter), **#187**.

---

**471. WHEN A TOKEN LOOKUP 404s, CHECK THE REQUEST'S ACTUAL TOKEN BEFORE YOU CHECK THE CODE (2026-09-08).**

`/onboarding-meeting` reported *"Invalid or expired link"* during testing and the handler was searched for a bug it did not have. The cause was that the URL had been copied out of a **chat message where the token was displayed truncated with an ellipsis** (`token=86f970d4...`), and the ellipsis was pasted along with the prefix.

A token page cannot tell "no such token" from "a token you mangled" — both are zero rows, both render the same card, and that is correct behaviour, not a diagnostic gap to close. **So the first move on any token 404 is to print the token the server actually received and compare its LENGTH to the minted one** (32 bytes hex = 64 chars here). Documentation, chat transcripts, terminal wrapping and email clients all truncate or re-wrap long opaque strings, and every one of them produces a string that still *looks* like a token.

Corollary for writing things down: when you paste an example URL into a doc or a message, either paste it whole or make it obviously unusable (`token=<token>`), never a real prefix with an ellipsis — someone will click it. Related: **#310** (the token row is the whole credential), **#295**.

---

**472. A COUNTDOWN LADDER MUST RE-ARM ON RESCHEDULE — INCLUDING THE RECORDED RESPONSE — WHICH IS THE EXACT OPPOSITE OF #404 (2026-09-04).**

**#404** says a resend must NOT re-arm a chase: the recipient has had the form all along, so re-stamping `*_sent_at` restarts a chase from zero for nothing. The preliminary-meeting reminder is the mirror case and takes the mirror rule.

Its ladder is a **countdown to an instant** — one business day out, then 60 minutes, then 10 minutes before `meeting_at`. When the meeting moves, every stamp describes a moment that no longer exists, so rescheduling **nulls `meeting_reminder_sent_at`, `meeting_reminder_60m_sent_at` and `meeting_reminder_10m_sent_at`** and lets the whole ladder run again against the new time. A forward ladder chases an unanswered ask; a countdown ladder chases a clock. Ask which kind you have before copying either behaviour.

**The half that is easy to miss is `meeting_response`.** The 60- and 10-minute tiers are gated on `meeting_response='confirm'`, so a reschedule that clears the stamps but keeps the confirmation would fire two countdown emails at a meeting time the prospect has never agreed to. Reschedule nulls `meeting_response` and `meeting_response_at` too: **the answer belonged to the old question.** Any gate column that records an answer *about the thing being rescheduled* is part of the re-arm, not merely part of the history.

**And a stopped row silences it entirely** — every tier filters `status='active'`, so a `No Show` recorded before the meeting time leaves the 60/10 tiers permanently unfired. That is correct, and it is also why those two tiers can look untested when the pipeline is behaving perfectly: the ladder was stopped, not broken. Related: **#404**, **#396/#397**, **#403**.

---

**473. ONE STRIPE CUSTOMER, TWO LEGS: EVERY WEBHOOK BRANCH KEYED ON THE CUSTOMER MUST SPLIT ON `payment_kind` FROM THE SESSION METADATA, AND EVERY GENERIC RESOLVER MUST SKIP THE NEW KINDS (2026-09-04).**

The membership deposit and the onboarding payment are two separate collections on **the same** `advisor_onboarding` row and the same Stripe customer. Before the deposit existed, "this customer's `checkout.session.completed`" unambiguously meant the onboarding payment, and several branches were written that way.

**Three things follow, and all three had to change together.**

**(1) The discriminator has to live on the SESSION metadata, not only the PaymentIntent.** In `checkout.session.completed` the PI has not been fetched yet — the session is all you have — so `session.metadata.payment_kind` is the only thing available at the moment the branch is chosen. Set the kind on **both** the session and the PI when you create the session: the session serves checkout, the PI serves `payment_intent.succeeded` and `payment_intent.payment_failed`. Setting it on only one of them leaves half the lifecycle undiscriminated.

**(2) The new branch goes AHEAD of the old one, and the old one keeps its own positive guard.** Ordering alone is not the fix — an unguarded legacy block below a guarded new one is one refactor away from catching the deposit again.

**(3) The generic failure resolver is the part you will forget.** The shared first-payment failure handler writes `payment_status`, which belongs to the onboarding payment. Left alone it would write that column for a bounced *deposit* — a real status, on the right row, describing the wrong money. It now tests `payment_kind` and skips both onboarding legs, which route to their own `handleOnboardingDepositFailure` / `handleOnboardingBalanceFailure`.

**The general rule: when a row gains a SECOND collection, enumerate every webhook branch and every shared resolver that identifies work by the row or the customer, and split each one explicitly.** None of them fails loudly when you miss it — they write a plausible value into a neighbouring column. This is #466's "give a new writer its own value" one level up, in the router. Related: **#466**, **#327**, **#287**, **#450**.

---

**474. `document_numbers` "EXACTLY ONE OWNER" MEANS A TEST ONBOARDING CANNOT BE UNLINKED FROM ITS NUMBERS — DELETE THE NUMBER ROWS (2026-09-08).**

Cleaning up test advisor onboardings 20-24 and accountant 30 failed on the `document_numbers_exactly_one_owner` CHECK: it requires **exactly one** of `client_id` / `advisor_onboarding_id` / `accountant_onboarding_id` / `specialist_onboarding_id` to be non-null, so the usual "null out the FK, keep the ledger row" cleanup is not available. The rows must be deleted.

**That is safe here, and it is worth knowing why, because "append-only sequence ledger" argues the opposite.** `allocateDocNumber` derives its candidate by **counting rows** and then retries past collisions, and the issued number **embeds the owner id** (`INV-ADV21-0050`), so a deleted test row can never be re-issued to a different owner — the id is gone with it. Deleting test rows therefore neither corrupts the sequence nor risks a duplicate.

The reusable shape: **a CHECK that enforces "exactly one owner" also enforces "no orphans", which turns every child row into a hard dependency of its parent's deletion.** Before designing such a constraint, decide what test-data cleanup looks like under it; before cleaning up, read the allocator to learn whether deleting is safe or merely convenient. Related: **#282**, **#206**, and the reset procedure in [operations/test-client-reset.md](operations/test-client-reset.md).

---

**475. UNPINNING `verification_method` TURNS `checkout.session.completed` INTO A *SUBMIT* EVENT — READ `payment_intent.status`, RECORD THE SUB-STATE IN A SIDE-COLUMN, SWAP THE COPY, AND HANDLE `payment_intent.canceled` (2026-09-08, `vfo-admin-api` v816). ALWAYS-APPLIES (Stripe / ACH / money-truth).**

`/pay` (`actions/pipeline/contract-stripe-checkout.ts`) and `/tax-pay` (`actions/tax/stripe-checkout.ts`) stopped pinning `payment_method_options[us_bank_account][verification_method]='instant'`, so Stripe's `automatic` default now offers **bank sign-in first with manual account/routing entry as the fallback** — the same widening **#298** made for SPECREV, for the same reason (`instant` locks out every bank Financial Connections does not support). The unpin is one line. **Everything below is what the one line costs, and every item is a place the system would otherwise have lied or stalled.**

**(1) `checkout.session.completed` no longer means paid.** On the manual-entry path Stripe fires it at SUBMIT with the PaymentIntent parked in `requires_action` (micro-deposits pending, **no debit attempted**). The branch already fetched that PI to read `payment_method` and was throwing `status` away — exactly the shape of **#370** on the one-time SpecRev path. **If you take the fallback, you must read the PI's own status; the session's arrival is not evidence of a payment.**

**(2) Do NOT add a status value — use a side-column.** `pay1_status` / `retainer_status` / `final_retainer_status` keep saying `'processing'` throughout the wait, and the new sub-state lives in three nullable `timestamptz` columns (`pay1_bank_verification_pending_at`, `retainer_bank_verification_pending_at`, `final_retainer_bank_verification_pending_at`, migration `20260908120000_…`). This is the deliberate opposite of the SpecRev choice, whose fourth value `awaiting_verification` immediately stranded the settle path because one guard read `=== 'processing'` (**#371**). A side-column is invisible to code that does not read it, so the settle branches, the late-ACH failure branches, the charge sweep, the open-installment resolver, the refund guards and the frontend status maps all needed **zero** changes. **The rule: when a new sub-state does not change what the row OWES, do not put it in the status column.**

**(3) Swap the client copy, or you send a lie.** `CONTRACT_confirmationemail|ach` and `TAX_confirmationemail|ach` say *"we have received your payment"* — false when nothing has been debited. Four new `|ach_verify` rows (plain + member-paying twin each, recipients copied verbatim from the rows they twin, `send_mode=false`) are picked off the stamp, and the three "client paid" bells (`MAP1_client_paid`, `TAX_retainer_paid`, `TAX_retainer_paid_schedule_meeting`) re-word to *"submitted bank details (verification pending) … no money has moved yet"*. Reporting the INTENT as the OUTCOME is **#468** all over again. Tax's handler selects columns by name, so the discriminator had to be added to its `.select(...)` **in the same edit** or the predicate would be silently, permanently false (**#448**).

**(4) Micro-deposit expiry is `payment_intent.canceled`, NOT `payment_intent.payment_failed` — and it was handled for SpecRev only.** When the client never verifies, Stripe cancels after ~10 business days and emits `canceled`; a dashboard Cancel does the same. Without this the row sits at `'processing'` **forever**, with no bell and no client email. The whole `payment_failed` block now also runs for `canceled` when `metadata.pipeline === 'TAX'` or `metadata.payment_number` is set, with `failReason` reading `cancellation_reason`. **Whenever you expose a path that can expire, find the event the expiry actually emits — do not assume it is the failure event you already consume.** *(NARROWED 2026-09-09 by #482 — a `canceled` fails a row only when that row is already `'processing'`; an untouched row means an abandoned Checkout page, not an expired verification.)*

**(5) A P1 PaymentIntent carries `payment_number = '1'`, which made the generic first-payment resolver skip it.** `isOffSession` is `!!md.payment_number || …`, written when only the sweep stamped that field; the client-driven `/pay` page started stamping it too (v773). So a cancelled Holistic P1 was classified as an off-session installment and resolved to nothing. `isFirstInstallmentCancel` (`canceled` **and** `payment_number === '1'`) removes it from `isOffSession` for that event only — a P1 **bounce** is still deliberately left to `checkout.session.async_payment_failed`, which fires the same bell title and dedupes. **A metadata field that two different producers set no longer means what its original consumer assumed.**

**(6) `custom_text[submit][message]` is the ONLY client-facing lever, and it supports `**bold**`.** Stripe renders it above the submit button, markdown and all (confirmed live). Stripe's own *"Enter bank details manually (may take 1-2 business days)"* link text belongs to Stripe and **cannot be edited or hidden**, and the fallback must stay available — so one bolded sentence asking people to prefer bank sign-in is the entire mitigation. It lives once, in `constants/ach-checkout-note.ts`, and the two recurring builders **prepend** it to their existing "no charge today" sentence rather than replacing it.

**Scope, deliberately.** Nine other builders (advisor, accountant, PIP, GC, membership setup, migration connect-checkout, background check, licence first-charge, card-update) still pin `instant` and were left alone — each would need its own version of items 1-5 first. **Accepted gaps:** a Tax IMPLEMENTATION retry through a fresh `/tax-pay` link books nothing at `checkout.session.completed`, so its link stays payable for the whole ~10-day wait rather than 2-4 days (same shape as the hub's *"a `/pay` ACH for N≥2"* item); and there is **no stall sweep** between submit and Stripe's cancel — SpecRev has one, here the checkout-time bell plus Stripe's own reminder emails are the coverage.
